A tree bijection for the moduli space of genus- 𝟎 \boldsymbol{0} hyperbolic surfaces with boundaries
Timothy Budd, Thomas Meeusen, Bart Zonneveld

Abstract
The Weil-Petersson volume of genus- g g hyperbolic surfaces with geodesic boundaries is known since work of Mirzakhani to be polynomial in the boundary lengths. We provide a bijective proof of this fact in the genus- 0 0 case in the presence of a distinguished cusp. It is based on a generalization of a recent tree bijection, by the first author and Curien, to the setting with geodesic boundaries, requiring an extension of the Bowditch-Epstein-Penner spine construction. As an application of our tree bijection we establish an explicit formula for the distance-dependent three-point function, which records an exact metric statistic measuring the difference of two geodesic distances among a triple of distinguished cusps in a Weil-Petersson random surface. We conclude with a discussion of the relevance of this function to the topological recursion of Weil-Petersson volumes and metric properties of Weil-Petersson random surfaces with many boundaries or cusps.



\useosf

Figure 1:
Illustration of a genus-
0
hyperbolic surface
\mathsf{X}
with cusps and geodesic boundaries (left). The bijection in this work considers the spine construction in the extended hyperbolic surface
\check{\mathsf{X}}
(right), obtained by attaching funnels to the geodesic boundaries of
\mathsf{X}
. The tree in question corresponds to the cut locus of points that have multiple length-minimizing geodesics to a horocycle around the distinguished cusp, called the origin.

1
Introduction

Many families of random metrics on a fixed topological surface have been studied in the literature using equally diverse mathematical techniques.
Since these families often share universal characteristics, or, in the language of physics, can each be viewed as a version of
two-dimensional quantum gravity
, it is natural to investigate whether any of the employed techniques are transferable.

One prominent family of models, with a long history in both mathematics and physics, is that of random combinatorial maps (or ribbon graphs).
The extensive toolkit used to study their enumeration, as well as statistical properties of uniformly sampled maps, includes analytic combinatorics, matrix integrals, integrable hierarchies, topological recursion, probabilistic exploration processes, and more.
Yet there is one family of techniques, intrinsic to the combinatorial structure of maps, that has proved particularly powerful in recent decades: the
bijective method
.
The idea is simple: a bijection between two combinatorial classes not only establishes their equinumeration, but also relates uniform random samples in both classes.

This is particularly effective when one of the classes consists of (decorated) combinatorial trees, because many combinatorial and probabilistic methods are readily available to analyse them.
Starting with the bijections of Cori–Vauquelin
[
20
]
and Schaeffer
[
53
]
(and even earlier work of Mullin
[
47
]
), a rich collection of such tree bijections has been identified in the planar-map literature, see for instance
[
7
,
3
,
1
]
.
As we will discuss in more detail below, these tree bijections have played a central role in establishing scaling limits for the metric of large random planar maps, notably in the proofs of convergence to the Brownian sphere
[
37
,
41
]
.

The present work aims to transfer aspects of this bijective method from planar maps to the continuous setting of moduli spaces of planar hyperbolic surfaces equipped with their Weil-Petersson volume measures.
One may view the computation of Weil-Petersson volumes as a continuous analogue of the enumeration of maps, and the Weil-Petersson random hyperbolic surface as an analogue of the uniform random map.
The utility of a tree bijection in this context was already demonstrated by the first author and Curien
[
13
]
by proving that the Weil-Petersson punctured hyperbolic sphere has the same metric scaling limit, in the form of the Brownian sphere, as many models of large random planar maps (see below for discussions).
The main goal of this work is to extend the bijective method to planar hyperbolic surfaces with geodesic boundaries.

1.1
Main results

We start by explaining our main bijective result.
Recall that a hyperbolic surface with geodesic boundaries is a surface with a Riemannian metric of constant (Gaussian) curvature
-1
such that the boundaries are closed hyperbolic geodesics.
For
2g-2+n>0
and
\mathbf{L}=(L_{1},\ldots,L_{n})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n}
,
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}(\mathbf{L})
is the moduli space of hyperbolic surfaces with
n
labeled boundaries of specified lengths
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}
, viewed modulo orientation-preserving (and boundary preserving) isometries.
Some (or all) of the boundaries are allowed to have zero length, in which case they correspond to cusps.
The moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}(\mathbf{L})
comes with a natural volume measure
\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathrm{WP}}
arising from its Weil-Petersson symplectic structure.
The volume of the full moduli space is finite and known as the Weil-Petersson volume
V_{g,n}(\mathbf{L})=\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathrm{WP}}(\mathcal{M}_{g,n}(\mathbf{L}))
.

In this work we focus exclusively on the spherical case
g=0
and we will always assume that the first boundary is a cusp, so that the moduli space of interest is
\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}(0,\mathbf{L})
for
n\geq 2
and
\mathbf{L}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n}
.
This special cusp will be called the
origin
and the remaining boundaries will be considered to be labeled
1,\ldots,n
.

Figure 2:
Example of the tree
\mathfrak{t}
associated to a surface
\mathsf{X}\in\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}(\mathbf{L})
, with in this case
n=10
boundaries (of which
6
are cusps and
4
are geodesic) besides the origin cusp. The spine of the extended hyperbolic surface
\check{\mathsf{X}}
has the structure of a bicolor plane tree
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}
.

Tree bijection.

Inspired by the spine construction of Bowditch & Epstein
[
11
]
and Penner
[
48
]
, already employed in
[
13
]
, as well as tree bijections for planar maps (see the discussion in Section
1.2
), it is natural to consider the
spine
or
cut-locus
associated to the origin in a hyperbolic surface
\mathsf{X}\in\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}(0,\mathbf{L})
.
This is the subset of points of
\mathsf{X}
that have more than one length-minimizing geodesic to the origin.
Due to the spherical topology the spine generally has the structure of a forest, i.e. a disjoint union of real trees.
To turn it into a useful combinatorial tree, one should consider the spine in the extended hyperbolic surface
\check{\mathsf{X}}
obtained by attaching infinite hyperbolic funnels to
\mathsf{X}
(Figure
1
).
As will be explained in detail in Section
2
, joining the endpoints of the infinite arcs of the spine that run into the same funnel, gives the spine the combinatorial structure of a plane tree
\mathfrak{t}
, see Figure
2
.
This tree together with a decoration by real labels, carefully chosen to make the encoding bijective, is denoted
\mathsf{Spine}(\mathsf{X})
.

To make this precise, we consider the collection
\mathfrak{T}_{n}
of (red and white) bicolored plane trees
\mathfrak{t}
with
n
white vertices labeled
1,\ldots,n
, also called
boundary
vertices, and an arbitrary number of red vertices of degree
3
, called
inner
vertices.
We let
\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})
,
\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})
and
\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})
be the set of inner vertices, boundary vertices and edges of
\mathfrak{t}
.
We also denote by
\vec{\mathsf{E}}(\mathfrak{t})
the oriented edges and for convenience we denote by
\vec{\mathsf{e}},\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}\in\vec{\mathsf{E}}(\mathfrak{t})
the two orientations of an edge
\mathsf{e}\in\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})
.
We write
\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},1},\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},2},\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},3}\in\vec{\mathsf{E}}(\mathfrak{t})
respectively
\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{b},1},\ldots,\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{b},\deg(\mathsf{b})}\in\vec{\mathsf{E}}(\mathfrak{t})
for the sequence of oriented edges starting at an inner vertex
\mathsf{v}
, respectively boundary vertex
\mathsf{b}
, where it is assumed that at each vertex a counterclockwise ordering of the edges is chosen in an arbitrary deterministic fashion.
If
\mathbf{L}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n}
, we will write
L_{\mathsf{b}}=L_{i}
in case the boundary vertex carries label
i
.
To properly accommodate cusps among the boundaries, we further introduce the restricted family of trees
\mathfrak{T}_{n}(\mathbf{L})\subset\mathfrak{T}_{n}
, by requiring
\deg(\mathsf{b})=1
in case
L_{\mathsf{b}}=0
.

To
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
we associate a
(2n-4)
-dimensional polytope
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})\subset\mathbb{R}^{6n-6}
as follows.
For
k\geq 1
and
y>0
we denote by
\Delta_{k}^{(y)}\coloneqq\{\mathbf{x}\in(0,\infty)^{k}:x_{1}+\cdots+x_{k}=y\}
the open
(k-1)
-dimensional simplex of size
y
, and let
\Delta_{1}^{(0)}=\{0\}
by convention.
Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})\coloneqq\left\{\varphi:\vec{\mathsf{E}}(\mathfrak{t})\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\middle|\begin{array}[]{l}\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{b},i})=0\text{ for }\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t}),1\leq i\leq\deg(\mathsf{b}),\\
\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},i})>0\text{ for }\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t}),1\leq i\leq 3,\\
\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},1})+\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},2})+\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},3})=\pi\text{ for }\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t}),\\
\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}})+\varphi(\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu})<\pi\text{ for }\mathsf{e}\in\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})\end{array}\right\}\times\prod_{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})}\left(\Delta^{(L_{\mathsf{b}}/2)}_{\deg(\mathsf{b})}\right)^{2}.

This polytope is naturally equipped with a volume measure given by
2^{n-2}
times the
(2n-4)
-dimensional Lebesgue measure

\displaystyle\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{t}}=2^{n-2}\prod_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})}\mathrm{d}\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},1})\mathrm{d}\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},2})\prod_{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})}\prod_{j=1}^{\deg(\mathsf{b})-1}\mathrm{d}w_{\mathsf{b},j}\mathrm{d}v_{\mathsf{b},j}.

(5)

Here
(w_{\mathsf{b},1},\ldots,w_{\mathsf{b},\deg(\mathsf{b})},v_{\mathsf{b},1},\ldots,v_{\mathsf{b},\deg(\mathsf{b})})\in\left(\Delta^{(L_{\mathsf{b}}/2)}_{\deg(\mathsf{b})}\right)^{2}
parametrize the pair of simplices associated to boundary vertex
\mathsf{b}
.
Our main bijective result can then be formulated as follows.

Theorem 1
.

For
n\geq 2
, there exists an open subset
\mathcal{M}^{\circ}_{0,n+1}(0,\mathbf{L})\subset\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}(0,\mathbf{L})
of full Weil-Petersson measure and a bijection

\displaystyle\mathsf{Spine}:\mathcal{M}^{\circ}_{0,n+1}(0,\mathbf{L})\to\bigsqcup_{\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}(\mathbf{L})}\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})

such that the pushforward measure
\mathsf{Spine}_{*}\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathrm{WP}}
agrees with
\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{t}}
on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})
.

In fact, we will see in Theorem
7
that
\mathsf{Spine}
makes sense a bijection for the full moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}(0,\mathbf{L})
by considering an extended family of trees. But the polytopes associated to these extra trees are of dimension smaller than
2n-4
and therefore do not contribute to the volume.

As a direct consequence of Theorem
1
we obtain a bijective interpretation, in the special case of
g=0
with the first boundary a cusp, of the well-known fact
[
42
, Theorem 1.1]
due to Mirzakhani that
V_{g,n}(\mathbf{L})
is a rational homogeneous polynomial of degree
3g-3+n
in
\pi^{2},L_{1}^{2},\ldots,L_{n}^{2}
.

Corollary 2
.

For
n\geq 2
, the Weil–Petersson volume
V_{0,n+1}(0,\mathbf{L})
is the rational homogeneous polynomial of degree
n-2
in
\pi^{2},L_{1}^{2},\ldots,L_{n}^{2}
given by the finite sum

\displaystyle V_{0,n+1}(0,\mathbf{L})=\sum_{\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}(\mathbf{L})}\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})),\qquad\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L}))\in\pi^{2|\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})|}\cdot\prod_{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})}L_{\mathsf{b}}^{2\deg(\mathsf{b})-2}\cdot\mathbb{Q}.

(6)

Proof.

For each
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
, the volume
\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L}))
factorizes over the Cartesian product in (
1.1
).
The angular part is a rational polytope of dimension
2|\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})|
rescaled by
\pi
, while the simplex
\Delta^{(L_{\mathsf{b}}/2)}_{\deg(\mathsf{b})}
is a rational polytope of dimension
\deg(\mathsf{b})-1
rescaled by
L_{\mathsf{b}}
.
Therefore
\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L}))
is a rational monomial of degree
|\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})|+\sum_{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})}(\deg(\mathsf{b})-1)=n-2
in
\pi^{2},L_{1}^{2},\ldots,L_{n}^{2}
.
∎

Computing the volumes
\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L}))
is not entirely straightforward, due to the
Delaunay condition

\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}})+\varphi(\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu})<\pi
for
\mathsf{e}\in\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})
.
Luckily it turns out that the computation simplifies drastically if one uses an inclusion-exclusion principle to reverse the inequality, meaning that one instead considers the
anti-Delaunay condition

\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}})+\varphi(\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu})>\pi
for some edges.
The result is that the contributions to the volume in (
6
) can be repackaged in terms of an adapted collection of trees, that we call
anti-Delaunay trees
.
They form a superset
\widetilde{\mathfrak{T}}_{n}(\mathbf{L})\supset\mathfrak{T}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
of the bicolor trees introduced above, the only difference being that
\mathfrak{t}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{T}}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
is allowed to have inner vertices of arbitrary degree
3
or larger.

Figure 3:
Left: Example of an anti-Delaunay tree
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{T}}_{12}(\mathbf{L})
. Middle: The contribution to the Weil-Petersson volume for several low-degree vertices. Right: all five trees in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{T}}_{4}(\mathbf{L})
.

Theorem 3
.

For
n\geq 2
and
\mathbf{L}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n}
, the Weil-Petersson volume
V_{0,1+n}(0,\mathbf{L})
can be expressed as a sum over anti-Delaunay trees as

\displaystyle V_{0,1+n}(0,\mathbf{L})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathfrak{t}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{T}}_{n}(\mathbf{L})}\prod_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})}\frac{2^{\deg(\mathsf{v})-2}}{(\deg(\mathsf{v})-1)!}\,\gamma_{\deg(\mathsf{v})-1}\prod_{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})}\frac{2^{\deg(\mathsf{b})-2}}{(\deg(\mathsf{b})-1)!}\,t_{\deg(\mathsf{b})-1}(L_{\mathsf{b}}),

(7)

where

\displaystyle\gamma_{k}=\frac{(-1)^{k}\pi^{2k-2}}{(k-1)!},\quad t_{k}(L)=\frac{2}{k!}\left(\frac{L}{2}\right)^{2k}.

(8)

See Figure
3
for an illustration of
V_{0,4}(0,L_{1},L_{2},L_{3})=2\pi^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}(L_{1}^{2}+L_{2}^{2}+L_{3}^{2})
.

Readers familiar with intersection numbers on the moduli space of curves and the KdV hierarchy, may recognize
t_{k}
as the
times
appearing in the KdV differential equations and the
time shifts

\gamma_{k}
originating from expressing the cohomology class of the Weil-Petersson symplectic form in terms of
\psi
-classes
[
57
]
.
See Mirzakhani’s proof of Witten’s conjecture
[
43
]
and
[
39
,
46
,
38
]
, or the overview in
[
12
, Section 4]
.

Generating functions.

In this context it is well-known that the genus-
0
intersection numbers, and therefore also the genus-
0
Weil-Petersson volumes, are completely determined by the
string equation

[
57
,
34
]
.
As a consequence of Theorem
3
, our bijection allows to reinterpret the string equation as the characteristic equation for the generating function of the combinatorial anti-Delaunay trees
\widetilde{\mathfrak{T}}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
.

To make this precise, it is necessary to switch from working at fixed number of boundaries to generating functions of Weil-Petersson volumes.
Since the Weil-Petersson volumes depend on an
n
-tuple of boundary lengths, some care is required to formalize a notion of generating function.
A versatile definition was given by the first and third author in
[
15
, Section 1.4]
.
Informally, the generating function
F_{g}[\mu]
of genus-
g
Weil-Petersson volumes is the formal power series

\displaystyle F_{g}[\mu]=\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{1}{n!}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n}}V_{g,n}(\mathbf{L})\mathrm{d}\mu(L_{1})\cdots\mathrm{d}\mu(L_{n}),

(9)

which has a functional dependence
1
1

1

Using physicist’s notational conventions square brackets indicate functional dependence.
on a
weight

\mu
, which one may think of as a measure on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
or, more generally, as a linear function on the ring of even, real polynomials (see Section
4.2
for details).
For us the important generating function is that of double-cusped hyperbolic spheres

\displaystyle R[\mu]=\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n!}\int V_{0,2+n}(0,0,\mathbf{L})\,\mathrm{d}{\mu(L_{1})}\dots\mathrm{d}{\mu(L_{n})}=\int\mathrm{d}\mu(L_{1})+O(\mu^{2}),

(10)

which can also be understood as a second derivative of
F_{0}[\mu]
, see also
[
12
,
15
]
.
Theorem
3
translates into a bijective proof of the known string equation
[
15
, Equation (21)]
for
R[\mu]
.

Corollary 4

(String equation)
.

This generating function satisfies the
string equation

\displaystyle R[\mu]

\displaystyle=\sum_{d\geq 0}\frac{2^{d-1}}{d!}\left(t_{d}[\mu]+\mathbf{1}_{\{d\geq 2\}}\gamma_{d}\right)R[\mu]^{d},\quad t_{k}[\mu]\coloneqq\int t_{k}(L)\mathrm{d}\mu(L).

(11)

or equivalently
Z(R[\mu];\mu]=0
, where

\displaystyle Z(r;\mu]=\frac{\sqrt{r}}{\sqrt{2}\pi}J_{1}\left(2\pi\sqrt{2r}\right)-\int I_{0}\left(L\sqrt{2r}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu(L)}

(12)

is a formal power series in
r
involving the Bessel functions
J_{1}
and
I_{0}
.

As explained in detail in
[
15
]
, the generating function
R[\mu]
plays a central role in the topological recursion of Weil-Petersson volumes of hyperbolic surfaces with weights on the boundaries.
For an introduction to topological recursion
[
26
]
we refer the reader to
[
24
,
25
,
4
]
.
To be precise, we considered the formal power series defined in terms of
R[\mu]
and
Z(r;\mu]
via

\displaystyle\eta(u;\mu]=\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{u^{2p}}{(2p+1)!!}\frac{\partial^{p+1}Z}{\partial r^{p+1}}(R[\mu];\mu]=\frac{\sin(2\pi u)}{2\pi u}+\int\frac{\cos(2\pi u)-\cosh(L_{1}u)}{u^{2}}\mathrm{d}\mu(L_{1})+O(\mu^{2})

(13)

Then the invariants
\omega_{g,n}(\mathbf{u})
of the spectral curve

\displaystyle\begin{cases}x(u)=u^{2},\\
y(u)=2u\,\eta(u;\mu],\end{cases}

(14)

in the sense of
[
26
]
, were shown to be related to generating functions of Weil-Petersson volumes of genus-
g
surfaces with
n
distinguished boundaries and an arbitrary number of extra weighted boundaries.
To be precise, according to
[
15
, Lemma 20]
we have the formal power series identity

\displaystyle\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{p!}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n+p}}V_{g,n+p}(\mathbf{L},\mathbf{K})\prod_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}e^{-z_{i}L_{i}}\mathrm{d}L_{i}\prod_{j=1}^{p}\mathrm{d}\mu(K_{j})=\omega_{g,n}(\mathbf{u})\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{z_{i}}{u_{i}},\qquad u_{i}\coloneqq\sqrt{z_{i}^{2}-2R[\mu]}.

(15)

Upon setting the weight
\mu
to zero, one finds
R[0]=0
and
\eta(u;0]=\sin(2\pi u)/(2\pi u)
, this reduces to the standard Mirzakhani recursion of Weil-Petersson volumes
[
27
,
42
]
.

Metric space statistics.

Paralleling a similar phenomenon in planar map combinatorics
[
9
,
10
]
, it was shown in
[
15
, Theorem 2]
that this generalization of Mirzakhani’s recursion is most naturally phrased in the setting of hyperbolic surfaces with certain “tight” boundaries.
Based on an, as of yet heuristic, geometric interpretation via a “tight” version of the pants decomposition underlying the result of Mirzakhani, it was conjectured in
[
15
, Section 1.6]
that the spectral curve hides precise metric statistics (see also Section
1.2
).
To be precise, for
n\geq 0
,
\mathbf{L}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n}
, we let
\mathsf{X}\in\mathcal{M}_{0,3+n}(0,0,0,\mathbf{L})
be a triply-cusped hyperbolic sphere and consider the unique unit-length horocycles
h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}
around these cusps.
Denoting the hyperbolic metric on
\mathsf{X}
by
d_{\mathsf{X}}:\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we let the
distance difference
be
D(\mathsf{X})=d_{\mathsf{X}}(h_{1},h_{2})-d_{\mathsf{X}}(h_{1},h_{3})
, see Figure
4
.

Figure 4:
A hyperbolic sphere
\mathsf{X}
with three distinguished cusps equipped with their unit-length horocycles
h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}
. The distance difference
D(\mathsf{X})
measures the length difference between the two length-minimizing geodesics.

This defines a continuous mapping

\displaystyle D:\mathcal{M}_{0,3+n}(0,0,0,\mathbf{L})\to\mathbb{R}.

(16)

We may thus consider the push-forward
D_{*}\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathrm{WP}}
of the Weil-Petersson measure to
\mathbb{R}
, which for
n>0
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
with a density that we denote
X_{n}(x;\mathbf{L})\mathrm{d}x
.
We let
\hat{X}_{n}(u;\mathbf{L})
be its two-sided Laplace transform,

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{n}(u;\mathbf{L})\coloneqq\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{2ux}X_{n}(x;\mathbf{L})\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathcal{M}_{0,3+n}(0,0,0,\mathbf{L})}e^{2uD(\mathsf{X})}\mathrm{d}\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathrm{WP}}(\mathsf{X}).

(17)

It was predicted in
[
15
, Section 1.6]
that the generating function of
\hat{X}_{n}(u;\mathbf{L})
, which we call the
distance-dependent three-point function
, is directly related to the spectral curve (
14
).
With the help of the tree bijection in this paper we can settle this conjecture.

Theorem 5

(distance-dependent three-point function)
.

The formal power series

\displaystyle\hat{X}(u;\mu]=\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{1}{n!}\int\hat{X}_{n}(u;\mathbf{L})\mathrm{d}\mu(L_{1})\cdots\mathrm{d}\mu(L_{n}).

(18)

is related to (
13
) via

\displaystyle\hat{X}(u;\mu]=\frac{\sin(2\pi u)}{2\pi u\,\eta(u;\mu]}.

(19)

As far as we are aware, this constitutes the first exact global metric space statistic for Weil-Petersson random surfaces of fixed genus and fixed number of boundaries.
Many works deal with statistics of lengths of closed geodesics on such surfaces
[
44
,
31
]
, but such geodesics are not guaranteed to be geodesics in the metric space sense of being (concatenations of a few) length-minimizing curves.
An exception
2
2

2

A pair of points positioned oppositely on a systole of length
L
are necessarily at hyperbolic distance
L/2
, contrary to the case of a typical closed geodesic.
is the length of the
systole
, i.e. the shortest closed geodesic on the surface, but exact statistics are only known in large-
g
and/or large-
n
limits
[
45
,
32
]
.
The Gromov-Hausdorff convergence of random genus-
0
hyperbolic surfaces with cusps to the Brownian sphere, shown by Curien and the first author in
[
13
]
(see below), allows to import a host of known metric space statistics from the latter, but again only in the large-
n
limit.
Finally, we note that some metric space statistics arising from lengths of “tight” closed geodesics can be extracted from the already mentioned work
[
15
]
, see also
[
14
]
.

To illustrate the non-trivial content of the identity (
19
), let us examine the case
n=0,1
.
With the help of (
10
) and (
13
), we see that to first order in
\mu
this power series is given by

\displaystyle\hat{X}(u;\mu]=1+\int\frac{2\pi}{u}\frac{\cosh(L_{1}u)-\cos(2\pi u)}{\sin(2\pi u)}\mathrm{d}\mu(L_{1})+\cdots.

(20)

The constant term
\hat{X}_{0}(u)=1
for
n=0
reflects that the triply-punctured sphere deterministically satisfies
d_{\mathrm{hyp}}(h_{1},h_{2})=d_{\mathrm{hyp}}(h_{1},h_{3})
.
For
n=1
, one can perform the inverse Laplace transform of the integrand to find the explicit distribution for the distance difference on a hyperbolic surface with three cusps and a single boundary of length
L_{1}\geq 0
,

\displaystyle X_{1}(x;L_{1})=2\log\left(\frac{\cosh(x)+\cosh(L_{1}/2)}{\cosh(x)-1}\right).

Obtaining a similar closed form
X_{2}(x;L_{1},L_{2})
already appears to be challenging.

Theorem
5
can also be used to derive asymptotics as
n\to\infty
.
Let us demonstrate this for the special case of the random punctured sphere
\mathcal{S}_{n}\in\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}(\mathbf{0})
sampled with probability measure
\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathrm{WP}}/V_{0,n+1}(\mathbf{0})
, considered in
[
32
,
13
]
.
As a consequence of Theorem
5
we prove the following.

Corollary 6
.

If
\mathcal{S}_{n}
is the Weil-Petersson random
(n+1)
-cusped sphere, then the variance of the distance difference satisfies the asymptotics

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\operatorname{Var}\left(\frac{D(\mathcal{S}_{n})}{c_{\mathrm{WP}}\,n^{1/4}}\right)=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{8}},\qquad c_{\mathrm{WP}}=\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{3c_{0}}}=2.3392\ldots,

(21)

where
c_{0}
is the first positive zero of the Bessel function
J_{0}
.

According to
[
13
, Theorem 2]
the random surface
\mathcal{S}_{n}
equipped with the normalized hyperbolic metric
n^{-1/4}d_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
converges to a constant multiple of the Brownian sphere
[
40
,
36
,
41
]
.
In fact, Corollary
6
follows directly from
[
13
, Proposition 40]
once we know that
\sqrt{\pi/8}
is the variance of the distance difference for a triple of uniform points in the standard Brownian sphere.
The latter is easily verified: if
(\mathbf{e},Z)
is the Brownian snake (see e.g.
[
36
]
), then this variance is equal to
\mathbb{E}[(Z_{a}-Z_{b})^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[d_{\mathbf{e}}(a,b)]
for two uniform points
a
and
b
, where
d_{\mathbf{e}}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the pseudometric of the continuum random tree encoded by the Brownian excursion
\mathbf{e}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Hence,
\mathbb{E}[d_{\mathbf{e}}(a,b)]=\sqrt{\pi/8}
is equal to the expected area under the Brownian excursion
\mathbf{e}
, see for instance
[
33
, Section 2]
and the references there for the value
\sqrt{\pi/8}
.
The virtue of our new proof of Corollary
6
is that it provides an alternative, concise derivation
3
3

3

Compare to
[
13
, Section 6.6]
, where the main technical challenge in computing
c_{\mathrm{WP}}
came down to establishing the asymptotic variance of a particular Markov chain on
[0,\pi)
. Of course, the most technical part of our derivation is hidden in the proof of Theorem
5
.
of the scaling constant
c_{\mathrm{WP}}=\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{3c_{0}}}
.

Proof of Corollary
6
.

Taking the weight to be
\mu=x\delta_{0}
, with
\delta_{0}
the delta measure at zero, turns
\hat{X}(u;\mu]
into an exponential generating function of
\hat{X}_{n}(u;\mathbf{0})
with variable
x
.
Theorem
5
implies that the variance of the distance difference between a uniform triple of unit-length horocycles is given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}D(\mathcal{S}_{n})=\frac{\hat{X}_{n}^{\prime\prime}(0;\mathbf{0})}{4\hat{X}_{n}(0;\mathbf{0})}=\frac{[x^{n}]\hat{X}^{\prime\prime}(0;x\delta_{0}]}{4[x^{n}]\hat{X}(0;x\delta_{0}]}.

(22)

It follows from (
19
) and (
13
) that

\displaystyle\hat{X}(0;x\delta_{0}]=\frac{1}{M_{0}(x)},\qquad\hat{X}^{\prime\prime}(0;x\delta_{0}]=-\frac{2M_{1}(x)}{3M_{0}(x)^{2}}-\frac{4\pi^{2}}{3}\hat{X}(0;x\delta_{0}],\qquad M_{p}(x)\coloneqq\frac{\partial^{p+1}Z}{\partial r^{p+1}}(R[x\delta_{0}];0].

(23)

By
[
14
, Proposition 4.3]
, see also
[
13
, Lemma 6]
, the series
M_{p}(x)
have the same radius of convergence
x_{c}>0
and

\displaystyle M_{1}(x)\sim-\left(\frac{4\pi^{2}}{c_{0}}\right)^{2}x_{c},\qquad\frac{M_{0}(x)^{2}}{2M_{1}(x)}\sim x-x_{c}\qquad\text{as }x\to x_{c}.

Hence

\displaystyle\hat{X}(0;x\delta_{0}]\sim\frac{c_{0}}{4\pi^{2}\sqrt{2x_{c}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{x_{c}-x}},\quad\hat{X}^{\prime\prime}(0;x\delta_{0}]\sim\frac{1}{3}\frac{1}{x_{c}-x}\qquad\text{as }x\to x_{c}.

By standard transfer theorems and the
\Delta
-analyticity of
M_{0}(x)
and
M_{1}(x)
(see
[
13
, Lemma 6]
), we obtain the asymptotic estimate

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}D(\mathcal{S}_{n})=\frac{[x^{n}]\hat{X}^{\prime\prime}(0;x\delta_{0}]}{4[x^{n}]\hat{X}(0;x\delta_{0}]}

\displaystyle\mathrel{{\mathop{\sim}\limits}}\frac{\frac{1}{3}x_{c}^{-n-1}}{4\frac{c_{0}}{4\pi^{2}\Gamma(1/2)\sqrt{2n}}x_{c}^{-n-1}}=\frac{4\pi^{2}}{3c_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{\pi n}{8}},

(24)

from which the claim follows by rearranging.
∎

1.2
Discussion

Relation with planar maps.

Like in the previous works
[
12
,
15
,
13
]
, the presented results are largely inspired by the combinatorics of maps (also known as ribbon graphs).
Here we treat the relation between Weil-Petersson volumes of hyperbolic surfaces and enumeration of maps mostly as an analogy, interpreting the Weil-Petersson measure as a continuous version of the counting measure of maps.
Nonetheless, we point out that there is more to it than an analogy, as already exemplified in the early nineties by Kontsevich’s proof of Witten’s conjecture
[
57
,
34
]
, which involved a precise relation between the combinatorics of (metric) maps and intersection numbers on the moduli spaces.
From the point of view of the Weil-Petersson measure this relation can be understood via a limit in which the boundary lengths of the hyperbolic surfaces become very large, such that the geometry of the surface literally starts resembling a metric map
[
23
,
2
,
55
]
.
In another direction, the moduli space of genus-
0
hyperbolic surfaces with cusps is known to be bijectively related to irreducible metric maps due to work of Rivin
[
51
,
52
]
, see
[
17
]
and the discussion in
[
12
]
.

Figure 5:
The Bouttier-Di Francesco-Guitter bijection relates bipartite planar maps with a distinguished vertex (shown here in blue),
n
labeled faces, which are properly bicolored plane trees with
n
labeled white vertices and red vertices that are decorated by the graph distances.

It was argued in
[
13
]
that the spine construction for punctured hyberbolic spheres is analogous to the classical Cori-Vauquelin-Schaeffer bijection
[
20
,
53
]
for planar quadrangulations, i.e. planar maps with all faces of degree
4
.
One may view the extension presented in this work to the case of hyperbolic spheres with geodesic boundaries as an analogue of the more general Bouttier-Di Francesco-Guitter (BDiFG) bijection
[
7
]
for planar maps with faces of arbitrary degree (see also
[
8
,
5
]
for the convenient dual perspective of slice decomposition).
Here the prescribed lengths of the geodesic boundaries replace the prescribed degrees of the faces of the map.

To appreciate the close analogy, let us phrase the BDiFG bijection in the case of bipartite planar maps in a fashion that resembles our context.
On one side we have the set
M_{n}(\mathbf{d})
of bipartite planar maps with
n
labeled faces of half-degrees
4
4

4

Faces of a bipartite map necessarily half even degree, so it is convenient to record half of the degree.

\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
and a distinguished vertex, called the origin.
They correspond bijectively to
mobiles
, which are (red/white) bipartite plane trees with
n
labeled white vertices of degree
d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}
and with red vertices that are decorated by a positive integer.
These red vertices correspond precisely to the vertices of the original map, except for the origin, and the decoration records the graph distance to the origin.
The only restrictions on the decoration, stemming from their origin as graph distances, is that
1
appears at least once and that the integer label of two consecutive red neighbours of a white vertex at most increases by
1
in counterclockwise direction.
See Figure
5
for an example, noting the similarity to Figure
2
.

The analogue of the double-cusped generating function (
10
) is the multivariate generating function

\displaystyle R(g_{1},g_{2},\ldots)=\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n!}\ \sum_{d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}\geq 1}|M_{1+n}(1,\mathbf{d})|\,g_{d_{1}}\cdots g_{d_{n}}

(25)

for bipartite planar maps with a distinguished vertex and a distinguished face of degree
2
, which can also be interpreted as a distinguished edge inflated to a bigon.
They are also known as elementary slices in the slice decomposition
[
8
,
5
]
.
According to
[
7
, Equation (2.5)]
, the characteristic equation for (rooted) mobiles implies the “string equation”

\displaystyle R=1+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}g_{k}\binom{2k-1}{k}R^{k},

(26)

which is the analogue of Corollary
4
.

Finally, we point the reader to
[
6
,
8
]
for a showcase on how to use these generating functions to establish graph distance statistics, in a similar vein as our Theorem
5
.
We note that the results for maps are more precise, since they provide control on individual distances instead of the distance difference that we address.
It is an interesting open problem to find an expression for the distance-dependent two-point function with control on
d_{\mathsf{X}}(h_{1},h_{2})
for doubly-cusped spheres
\mathsf{X}\in\mathcal{M}_{0,2+n}(0,0,\mathbf{L})
.

Cusp-less planar hyperbolic surfaces.

As discussed above, the geodesic boundaries and cusps of a hyperbolic surface can be viewed as the analogues of, respectively, the faces and vertices of a map.
However, one aspect of this analogy is broken: while every map contains at least one vertex, not every hyperbolic surface contains a cusp.
Since we require an origin cusp to define the spine, this prevents us from giving a bijective interpretation of the general genus-
0
Weil-Petersson volume
V_{0,n}(\mathbf{L})
where each boundary length is allowed to be positive.
Inspired by the works of Bouttier-Guitter-Miermont
[
9
,
10
]
for maps, the third author will close this gap in
[
59
]
by extending the spine construction to the “half-tight” hyperbolic cylinders introduced in
[
15
]
.

Scaling limits.

The tree bijection for punctured hyperbolic spheres based on the spine construction was vital for proving the scaling limit towards the Brownian sphere in
[
13
]
, mirroring probabilistic methods well-established for large random planar maps and their tree encodings
[
36
,
41
]
.
This work provides a more general tree bijection that is well suited to address similar scaling limit results for Weil-Petersson random surfaces with boundaries.
As explained in
[
15
, Section 1.3]
, it is natural to consider the genus-
g

Boltzmann hyperbolic surface
associated to a weight
\mu
in the form of a Borel measure on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
for which the partition function (
9
) is finite,
F_{g}[\mu]<\infty
.
It is the hyperbolic surface
\mathsf{X}
with a random number
n
of boundaries of random lengths
\mathbf{L}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n}
obtained by first sampling
(n,\mathbf{L})
with joint density
V_{g,n}(\mathbf{L})/(n!F_{g}[\mu])\mathrm{d}\mu(L_{1})\cdots\mathrm{d}\mu(L_{n})
and then, conditionally on
(n,\mathbf{L})
, sampling a Weil-Petersson random surface in
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}(\mathbf{L})
.
In the case of genus-
0
Boltzmann hyperbolic surface
\mathsf{X}
with a distinguished cusp, Theorem
1
implies that
\mathsf{Spine}(\mathsf{X})
is a random decorated tree
\mathfrak{t}
with a rather explicit law, so one can attempt
5
5

5

Although explicit, the law of the tree is more complicated than the pure cusp-case of
[
13
]
. However, there is also a significant simplification when
\mu
admits boundaries of positive length: the random tree naturally features an embedded single-type Galton-Watson process corresponding to genealogy of boundary vertices, whereas
[
13
]
had to resort to introducing certain cutpoints to identify such a process.
the same procedure as in
[
13
]
to establish a scaling limit for the random metric space.
If the weight
\mu
is not too heavy-tailed and one conditions on the number
n
of boundaries to be large, one would expect again to obtain the Brownian sphere and the correct scaling constant can be extracted from a calculation similar to Corollary
6
.
However, there is also the potential of witnessing different universality classes if
\mu
is taken to be heavy-tailed, namely the stable carpets and stable gaskets appearing in the limits of random maps with large face degrees
[
22
,
37
]
.
The necessary non-generic criticality criteria on
\mu
have already been investigated in the works
[
16
,
35
]
.

Figure 6:
For
\mathsf{X}\in\mathcal{M}_{0,3+n}(0,0,0,\mathbf{L})
, the horocycle log-ratio is given by
H(\mathsf{X})=\log(b/a)
where
a
and
b
are the lengths of segments of a horocycle around the first cusp determined by the shortest geodesics to the second and third cusp.

Topological recursion.

We mentioned that Theorem
5
should somehow be related to the generalization of Mirzakhani’s topological recursion introduced in
[
15
]
.
To give a hint why this might be the case, let us discuss a slightly different statistic on triply-cusped hyperbolic spheres.
Let us denote by
\mathcal{M}^{\circ^{\prime}}_{0,3+n}(0,0,0,\mathbf{L})\subset\mathcal{M}_{0,3+n}(0,0,0,\mathbf{L})
the full-measure subset of such surfaces that have unique length-minimizing geodesics from the second and third cusp to the first.
For
\mathsf{X}\in\mathcal{M}^{\circ^{\prime}}_{0,3+n}(0,0,0,\mathbf{L})
this pair of geodesics cuts a small horocycle
h_{1}
around the first cusp into two horocyclic segments of lengths
a,b>0
, say.
Then we let the
horocycle log-ratio
be
H(\mathsf{X})=\log(b/a)
, see Figure
6
.
We claim that it has the same distribution as the distance-difference
D(\mathsf{X})
, meaning that we may also identify

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{n}(u;\mathbf{L})=\int_{\mathcal{M}^{\circ^{\prime}}_{0,3+n}(0,0,0,\mathbf{L})}e^{2uH(\mathsf{X})}\mathrm{d}\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathrm{WP}}(\mathsf{X}).

(27)

The reason is that there exists a natural measure-preserving involution (modulo interchanging labels
2
and
3
) on
\mathcal{M}_{0,3+n}^{\circ^{\prime}}(0,0,0,\mathbf{L})
that interchanges the two statistics.
This involution amounts to cutting open the surface
\mathsf{X}
along the pair of geodesic into an
n
-holed quadrangle, and then regluing the sides in the unique other way to achieve a triply-cusped sphere
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
again.
The cusp formed by the two opposite vertices of the quadrangle receives labeled
1
and it is not too difficult to see that the gluing seams ending there are the unique shortest geodesics.
We refer to Figure
7
for a pictorial proof of the fact that
D(\mathsf{X})=H(\mathsf{X^{\prime}})
and vice versa.

Figure 7:
A pictorial proof of
D(\mathsf{X})=H(\mathsf{X}^{\prime})
. After cutting and regluing the unit-length horocycles
h_{2}
and
h_{3}
on
\mathsf{X}
are mismatched on
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
by a signed hyperbolic distance
D(\mathsf{X})
, as illustrated at the top in the upper-half plane representation. Moving
h_{2}
to align with
h_{1}
changes its length by a factor
e^{D(\mathsf{X})}
, so that
H(\mathsf{X}^{\prime})=\log(e^{D(\mathsf{X})}/1)=D(\mathsf{X})
.

One may interpret a triply-cusped sphere as a “tight” pair of pants, in the sense of
[
15
]
, with three boundaries of length
0
.
We note that the power series
\hat{X}(u;\mu]
provides control on the partitioning of the horocycle around the initial puncture that is reminiscent of the partitioning of the initial boundary underlying the Mirzakhani-McShane identity
[
42
]
.
In fact, it is possible to turn (
27
) into a statistic for tight pairs of pants by cutting and regluing
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
along the three shortest geodesics connecting the three cusps, similarly to what was done in
[
9
]
for the case of maps.
But detailing this is beyond the scope of this work.

2
Tree bijection for hyperbolic surfaces

In this section we prove the bijection part of Theorem
1
.
We do so by establishing a stronger bijective result for the full moduli space, which involves are larger class of combinatorial trees than the ones introduced above.

2.1
Bijection for the full moduli space

Denote by
\mathfrak{T}^{\mathrm{all}}_{n}
the collection of (red and white) bicolored plane trees
\mathfrak{t}
with
n
white boundary vertices labeled
1,\ldots,n
and a finite number of red inner vertices of degree at least
3
.
Moreover, each corner of a boundary vertex of
\mathfrak{t}
is labeled
ideal
or
non-ideal
.
Let
\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})
,
\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})
,
\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})
are the inner vertices, boundary vertices and edges of
\mathfrak{t}
as before, but now
\mathsf{C}(\mathfrak{t})
is the set of non-ideal corners of
\mathfrak{t}
only.
The number of non-ideal corners of
\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})
is denoted
\operatorname{nonid}(\mathsf{v})
.
For
\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})\cup\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})
the oriented edges starting at
\mathsf{v}
are
\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},1},\ldots,\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},\deg(\mathsf{v})}\in\vec{\mathsf{E}}(\mathfrak{t})
in counterclockwise order.
Similarly, the non-ideal corners of
\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})
are
\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{b},1},\ldots,\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{b},\operatorname{nonid}(\mathsf{b})}
.
For
\mathbf{L}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n}
we let
\mathfrak{T}^{\mathrm{all}}_{n}(\mathbf{L})\subset\mathfrak{T}^{\mathrm{all}}_{n}
be the subset of trees such that for each
i=1,\ldots,n
the boundary vertex
\mathsf{b}
with label
i
has exclusively ideal corners if and only if
L_{i}=0
.
One may check that these definitions are compatible with those in the introduction, in the sense that
\mathfrak{T}_{n}(\mathbf{L})\subset\mathfrak{T}^{\mathrm{all}}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
if for
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
we label each corner at the
i
th boundary vertex to be ideal or non-ideal depending on whether
L_{i}=0
or
L_{i}>0
respectively.

To
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}^{\mathrm{all}}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
we associate the polytope
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})
as follows.
Recall the notation
\Delta_{k}^{(y)}\coloneqq\{\mathbf{x}\in(0,\infty)^{k}:x_{1}+\cdots+x_{k}=y\}
for the open
(k-1)
-dimensional simplex of size
y
.
Then we set

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})\coloneqq

\displaystyle\left\{\varphi:\vec{\mathsf{E}}(\mathfrak{t})\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\middle|\begin{array}[]{l}\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{b},i})=0\text{ for }\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t}),1\leq i\leq\deg(\mathsf{b}),\\
\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},i})>0\text{ for }\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t}),1\leq i\leq\deg(\mathsf{v}),\\
\sum_{i=1}^{\deg(\mathsf{v})}\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},i})=\pi\text{ for }\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t}),\\
\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}})+\varphi(\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu})<\pi\text{ for }\mathsf{e}\in\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})\\
L_{\mathsf{b}}>0\end{subarray}}\left[\Delta_{\deg(\mathsf{b})}^{(L_{\mathsf{b}}/2)}\times\Delta_{\operatorname{nonid}(\mathsf{b})}^{(L_{\mathsf{b}}/2)}\right]\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})\\
L_{\mathsf{b}}=0\end{subarray}}\Delta^{(1)}_{\deg(\mathsf{b})}.

(31)

It is a polytope of dimension

\displaystyle\dim\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})}(\deg(\mathsf{v})-1)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})\\
L_{\mathsf{b}}>0\end{subarray}}(\deg(\mathsf{b})+\operatorname{nonid}(\mathsf{b})-2)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})\\
L_{\mathsf{b}}=0\end{subarray}}(\deg(\mathsf{b})-1)

\displaystyle=2n-4+\sum_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})}(3-\deg(\mathsf{v}))+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})\\
L_{\mathsf{b}}>0\end{subarray}}(\operatorname{nonid}(\mathsf{b})-\deg(\mathsf{b}))+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})\\
L_{\mathsf{b}}=0\end{subarray}}(1-\deg(\mathsf{b})),

(32)

where we used that
\mathfrak{t}
is a combinatorial tree and therefore

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})}(2-\deg(\mathsf{v}))+\sum_{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})}(2-\deg(\mathsf{b}))=2.

(33)

Since the summands in (
32
) are all non-positive, it follows that
\dim\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}\leq 2n-4
with equality if and only if
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
belongs to the subclass of trees introduced in Section
1.1
, meaning that each inner vertex has degree
3
, each boundary vertex corresponding to a cusp has degree
1
and each corner at a boundary vertex corresponding to a geodesic boundary is non-ideal.

The main result of this section will be the following.

Theorem 7
.

For
n\geq 2
and
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}\geq 0
, the Bowditch-Epstein-Penner spine constructions determines a bijection

\displaystyle\mathsf{Spine}:\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}(0,\mathbf{L})\to\bigsqcup_{\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}^{\mathrm{all}}_{n}(\mathbf{L})}\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L}).

2.2
Generalizing the Bowditch-Epstein-Penner spine construction

Let
\mathsf{X}\in\mathcal{M}_{0,n+1}(0,\mathbf{L})
be a genus-
0
hyperbolic surface with a distinguished cusp, that we call the
origin
, and
n\geq 2
additional boundaries of lengths
\mathbf{L}=(L_{1},\ldots,L_{n})
(or cusps whenever
L_{i}=0
).
The universal cover of
\mathsf{X}
can be represented as a convex domain
P
in the Poincaré upper-half plane
\mathbb{H}
.
Denote by
\Gamma
the Fuchsian group
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that
\mathsf{X}=P/\Gamma
.
It is convenient to consider the extended surface
\check{\mathsf{X}}=\mathbb{H}/\Gamma\supset X
, corresponding to the surface
\mathsf{X}
in which to each geodesic boundary of positive length we glue a
funnel
with a geodesic boundary of the same length.
The surface
\check{\mathsf{X}}
thus has a complete hyperbolic metric with a
boundary at infinity
corresponding to each geodesic boundary of
\mathsf{X}
, and
\mathsf{X}
corresponds to the convex core of
\check{\mathsf{X}}
.
A point on a boundary at infinity is called an
ideal point
, which can be regarded as a common endpoint of limiting parallel infinite geodesics running into the funnel.

Figure 8:
Illustration of the universal cover
P
of the surface
\mathsf{X}
. Note that a boundary geodesic of
\mathsf{X}
lifts to a geodesic side of
P
. The surface
\check{\mathsf{X}}=\mathbb{H}/\Gamma
is extended with funnels. The spine of
\check{\mathsf{X}}
is illustrated in red.

For any point
x\in\mathsf{X}
we can make sense of the shortest geodesics from
x
to the origin as follows.
Let
h
be a horocycle around the origin that separates
x
from the cusp and
d_{\mathsf{X}}(x,h)
the hyperbolic distance between
x
and
h
.
Let
w(x)
be the number of distinct geodesics from
x
to
h
of length
d_{\mathsf{X}}(x,h)
.
Since each of these geodesics meets
h
perpendicularly and thus continues into the cusp, the extended geodesics and their number
w(x)
is independent of the choice of horocycle
h
.

The
spine

\Sigma
of
\check{\mathsf{X}}
is defined as the subset of points with at least two shortest geodesics to the root,

\Sigma=\{x\in\check{\mathsf{X}}:w(x)\geq 2\}\subset\check{\mathsf{X}}.

(34)

The points with three or more geodesics will be called
inner vertices

V=\{x\in\check{\mathsf{X}}:w(x)\geq 3\}\subset\Sigma.

(35)

The following lemma is a variant of
[
11
, Lemma 2.2.1]
by Bowditch & Epstein.

Lemma 8
.

The spine
\Sigma
satisfies the following properties.

1.

V
is a finite set.

2.

\Sigma\setminus V=\{x\in\check{\mathsf{X}}:w(x)=2\}
consists of a finite union of open geodesic arcs. Tracking each arc in each direction it either ends at a point in
V
or at an ideal point inside a funnel.

3.

Each point in
x\in V
is the endpoint of exactly
w(x)\geq 3
arcs.

4.

The boundary at infinity of each boundary or cusp (except the origin) contains the endpoint of at least one arc.

5.

The compactified spine
\overline{\Sigma}
, obtained by joining the endpoints of all arcs ending on the boundary at infinity of a single funnel or cusp, is a tree.

Proof.

Let us fix a horocycle
h
around the origin that is short enough not to self-intersect and to be contained in
\mathsf{X}\subset\check{\mathsf{X}}
(by the collar lemma, considering the unit-length horocycle suffices).
Then
h
does not intersect the spine
\Sigma
and it lifts to a countable collection
\mathcal{C}
of disjoint (and non-nested) horocycles in
\mathbb{H}
.
Let
x\in\check{\mathsf{X}}
be a point separated from the origin by
h
and
y\in\mathbb{H}
a lift of
x
.
Then
w(x)
counts the number of horocycles in
\mathcal{C}
that are closest to
x
, say at distance
r
.
The number
w(x)
is necessarily finite, since every hyperbolic disk, in particular the ball of radius
r
around
y
, can meet only finitely many disjoint horodisks.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be such that there are still only
w(x)
horocycles in
\mathcal{C}
within distance
r+2\varepsilon
of
y
.
Then the shape of the spine can be established within an
\varepsilon
-neighborhood of
x
, i.e.
\Sigma\cap\mathsf{Ball}(x,\varepsilon)
.
This is particularly conveniently seen in the Poincaré disk
\mathbb{D}
when
y
is positioned at the origin (Fig.
9
).

Figure 9:
The first figure illustrates the collection
\mathcal{C}
of disjoint horocycles. The blue ones are at closest distance
r
from
y
, while the gray ones are at distance at least
r+2\varepsilon
. The other figures illustrate the spine neighbourhoods
\Sigma\cap\mathsf{Ball}(x,\varepsilon)
for different number
w(x)
.

In particular,
V
is locally finite and
\Sigma\setminus V
is composed of a locally-finite collection of geodesic arcs, and at least three arcs meet at every point of
V
.

Let
\mathsf{X}^{\circ}\subset\mathsf{X}\subset\check{\mathsf{X}}
be the compact surface obtained by removing the interiors of small horocycles around the cusps of
\mathsf{X}
.
It follows that
V\cap\mathsf{X}^{\circ}
is finite and
(\Sigma\setminus V)\cap\mathsf{X}^{\circ}
consists of finitely many geodesic arcs.
In particular, there are only finitely many points
\Sigma\subset\partial\mathsf{X}^{\circ}
on the boundary of
\mathsf{X}^{\circ}
with more than one shortest geodesic to
h
.
Now we examine a single funnel or horocyclic region of
\check{\mathsf{X}}\setminus\mathsf{X}^{\circ}
, which we can represent in
\mathbb{H}
with a hyperbolic respectively parabolic generator
g
.
It follows that the cut-locus in this region is determined by a non-zero finite number (up to translation by
g
) lifts of
h
.
From this is it follows easily that the cut-locus in this region consists of a finite number of geodesic arcs as well, and that at least one of the arcs ends on an ideal point.
Combined this proves the first four properties.

For the complement of the spine
\check{\mathsf{X}}\setminus\Sigma=\{x\in\check{x}:w(x)=1\}
we obtain a retraction onto the horodisk around the origin, by tracing the unique shortest geodesics to the origin.
Hence,
\check{\mathsf{X}}\setminus\Sigma
is topologically an open punctured disk.
This in turn implies that the compactified spine
\overline{\Sigma}
is connected and simply-connected, so must be a tree.
∎

We can rephrase this lemma as follows.

Lemma 9
.

Let
\mathfrak{t}
be the bicolored plane graph with red inner vertices
\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})=V
, white boundary vertices
\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})
corresponding to the
n
boundaries at infinity, and edge set
\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})
determined by the arcs in
\Sigma\setminus V
.
We designate each corner of a boundary vertex to be ideal respectively non-ideal depending on whether the two adjacent arcs in
\Sigma\setminus V
are limiting parallel (i.e. end on the same ideal point) respectively ultraparallel (i.e. end on distinct ideal points).
Then
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}^{\mathrm{all}}(\mathbf{L})
.

Proof.

Lemma
8
implies
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}^{\mathrm{all}}
.
Moreover, all arcs entering a cusp necessarily end on the same ideal point so the corners of the corresponding boundary vertex are exclusively ideal, and the converse holds as well.
This shows that
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}^{\mathrm{all}}(\mathbf{L})
.
∎

2.3
A canonical tiling of
\check{\mathsf{X}}

We will now show that, with the help of the spine
\Sigma
, we can canonically tile
\check{\mathsf{X}}
by hyperbolic triangles and wedges.
For any
x\in\check{\mathsf{X}}\setminus\Sigma
there exists a unique geodesic from
x
to the origin that does not intersect
\Sigma
.
Extending this geodesic beyond
x
, either it hits
\Sigma
for the first time at some point
\mathsf{end}(x)\in\Sigma
or it is an infinite geodesic disjoint from
\Sigma
approaching an ideal point
\mathsf{end}(x)
on a boundary at infinity.
By convention, we further let
\mathsf{end}(x)=x
for
x\in\Sigma
.

associated to a non-ideal corner
\mathsf{c}\in\mathsf{C}(\mathfrak{t})
. They are bounded by inner ribs and boundary ribs respectively.

\mathsf{e}\in\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})
with the corresponding arc in
\Sigma\setminus\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})
, so that we can associate to it the subset

\mathsf{e}
and two (open) hyperbolic triangles that share a side along
\mathsf{e}
, see Figure
10
.
By definition of the spine, these triangles have the same side lengths (with respect to a chosen horocycle at the origin) and therefore they are congruent via reflection in
\mathsf{e}
.

If
\mathsf{c}\in\mathsf{C}(\mathfrak{t})
is a non-ideal corner, the two neighbouring edges
\mathsf{e}
and
\mathsf{e}^{\prime}

\mathsf{c}
as well.
We then consider the subset

.

and quadrilaterals

{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.14 5.71 C 2.85 6.28 5.14 8.56 5.71 11.42 M 1.14 5.71 C 2.85 5.14 5.14 2.85 5.71 0 M 10.28 5.71 C 8.56 6.28 6.28 8.56 5.71 11.42 M 10.28 5.71 C 8.56 5.14 6.28 2.85 5.71 0 M 1.14 5.71 L 10.28 5.71}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}_{\mathsf{e}}
are called the
ribs
of
\check{\mathsf{X}}
.
We distinguish
inner ribs
and
boundary ribs
depending on whether their endpoint is in
V
or is an ideal point.
The union of ribs (including the points of
V
) is denoted
\mathsf{Ribs}(\check{\mathsf{X}})
.
We have thus established the following lemma.

Lemma 10
.

The surface
\check{\mathsf{X}}
partitions into

2.4
Tree labels

We need to establish which information to record together with the combinatorial tree
\mathfrak{t}
in order for the hyperbolic surface
\mathsf{X}
to be uniquely characterized.
The wedges
(\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to7.91pt{\vbox to8.62pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

have a unique (intrinsic) geometry, while the pairs of congruent triangles making up the quadrilaterals
(\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to7.4pt{\vbox to9.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-0.42513pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

are determined by their angles.
For
\mathsf{e}\in\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})
we let
\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}),\varphi(\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu})\in[0,\pi)
be the angle in either one of the triangles of
\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to7.4pt{\vbox to9.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-0.42513pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

at the corner corresponding to the starting point of
\vec{\mathsf{e}}
and

\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}

respectively.

Lemma 11
.

The angles
\varphi:\vec{\mathsf{E}}(\mathfrak{t})\to[0,\pi)
satisfy

(i)

\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}})=0
if and only if
\vec{\mathsf{e}}
starts at a boundary vertex;

(ii)

\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}})+\varphi(\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu})<\pi
for
\mathsf{e}\in\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})
;

(iii)

\sum_{j=1}^{\deg(\mathsf{v})}\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},j})=\pi
for
\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})
.

Proof.

The first property follows from the fact that the angle
\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}})
vanishes only when the arc starts at an ideal point, hence when
\vec{\mathsf{e}}
starts at a boundary vertex.
The second property is a consequence of the fact that angles in a hyperbolic triangle add up to less than
\pi
, while the last property stems from the fact that the angles around
v\in\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})
should add up to
2\pi
(and each angle
\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},j})
contributes twice because the quadrilateral consists of two congruent triangles).
∎

The combinatorial structure of
\mathfrak{t}
dictates which sides of the quadrilaterals and wedges are paired at the ribs of
\mathsf{X}
.
The inner ribs are half-infinite so there is no ambiguity in the gluing of the adjacent sides of the quadrilaterals.
The boundary ribs, however, are bi-infinite so there is a shearing degree of freedom in the gluing of the neighboring pair of quadrilaterals or quadrilateral-wedge pair.

Figure 11:
The neighbourhood of a funnel, tiled by hyperbolic quadrangles and wedges, can be conveniently represented in the upper-half plane
\mathbb{H}
by aligning the closed geodesic with the vertical axis.

To fix the gluing unambiguously, let us zoom in on the neighborhood of a funnel of perimeter
L_{\mathsf{b}}>0
associated to a boundary vertex
\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})
, meaning that
L_{\mathsf{b}}=L_{i}
if
\mathsf{b}
carries label
i
, see Figure
11
for an example where
\deg(\mathsf{b})=2
.
By convention we orient the closed geodesic of length
L_{\mathsf{b}}
, corresponding to the boundary of the original surface
\mathsf{X}
, so that the origin is on the left and the boundary at infinity on the right.
Viewed in the universal cover in the upper-half plane
\mathbb{H}
, we can conjugate the Fuchsian group
\Gamma
to assure that the geodesic is oriented upwards along the imaginary axis and the associated hyperbolic generator is
z\mapsto e^{L_{\mathsf{b}}}z
.
The boundary ribs ending at
\mathsf{b}
lift to a sequence of disjoint semicircles in
\mathbb{H}
that each intersect the imaginary axis.
For each
j=1,\ldots,\deg(\mathsf{b})
, if
r^{\prime}>r^{\prime}>0
are the radii corresponding to the ribs bounding the quadrilateral
\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to7.4pt{\vbox to9.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-0.42513pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}_{\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{b},j}}
, we set

\displaystyle w_{\mathsf{b},j}=\log\frac{r^{\prime}}{r}

(36)

to be the log-ratio.
Similarly, for each
j=1,\ldots,\operatorname{nonid}(\mathsf{b})
, we let
v_{\mathsf{b},j}=\log\frac{r^{\prime}}{r}
be the log-ratio of the radii
r^{\prime}>r>0
associated to the ribs that bound the wedge
\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to7.91pt{\vbox to8.62pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 8.86 C 2.95 4.92 3.44 5.91 4.92 0 M 4.92 0 C 6.4 5.91 6.89 4.92 9.84 8.86 M 9.84 8.86 C 4.92 10.83 4.92 10.83 0 8.86}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}_{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{b},j}}
.
Alternatively, we can associate to each boundary rib a marking on the boundary geodesic by projecting the top of the semicircle horizontally onto the vertical axis.
These markings give rise to a canonical partition of the boundary into intervals of hyperbolic length
w_{\mathsf{b},1},\ldots,w_{\mathsf{b},\deg{\mathsf{b}}},v_{\mathsf{b},1},\ldots,v_{\mathsf{b},\operatorname{nonid}(\mathsf{b})}
.

Lemma 12
.

If
\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})
is the boundary vertex with label
i
and
L_{\mathsf{b}}>0
, then these labels satisfy

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\deg(\mathsf{b})}w_{\mathsf{b},j}=\sum_{j=1}^{\operatorname{nonid}(\mathsf{b})}v_{\mathsf{b},j}=\frac{L_{\mathsf{b}}}{2},

(37)

and they uniquely determine the gluing of the quadrilaterals
\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to7.4pt{\vbox to9.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-0.42513pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.14 5.71 C 2.85 6.28 5.14 8.56 5.71 11.42 M 1.14 5.71 C 2.85 5.14 5.14 2.85 5.71 0 M 10.28 5.71 C 8.56 6.28 6.28 8.56 5.71 11.42 M 10.28 5.71 C 8.56 5.14 6.28 2.85 5.71 0 M 1.14 5.71 L 10.28 5.71}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}_{\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{b},j}}
and wedges
\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to7.91pt{\vbox to8.62pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 8.86 C 2.95 4.92 3.44 5.91 4.92 0 M 4.92 0 C 6.4 5.91 6.89 4.92 9.84 8.86 M 9.84 8.86 C 4.92 10.83 4.92 10.83 0 8.86}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}_{\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{b},j}}
incident to
\mathsf{b}
.

Proof.

Pairs of consecutive boundary ribs around
\mathsf{b}
are asymptotically parallel, on the right (in
\mathbb{H}
) if they bound the same quadrilateral and on the left if they bound the same wedge.
By construction, the radii of two lifts of a rib to
\mathbb{H}
that are related by
z\mapsto e^{L_{\mathsf{b}}}z
differ in radius by a factor

\displaystyle\exp\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\deg(\mathsf{b})}w_{\mathsf{b},j}+\sum_{j=1}^{\operatorname{nonid}(\mathsf{b})}v_{\mathsf{b},j}\right)=e^{L_{\mathsf{b}}}.

Another relation follows from examining the lifts of the horocycle
h
around the origin, shown in blue in Figure
11
.
Using that the sides of the quadrilateral
\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to7.4pt{\vbox to9.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-0.42513pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.14 5.71 C 2.85 6.28 5.14 8.56 5.71 11.42 M 1.14 5.71 C 2.85 5.14 5.14 2.85 5.71 0 M 10.28 5.71 C 8.56 6.28 6.28 8.56 5.71 11.42 M 10.28 5.71 C 8.56 5.14 6.28 2.85 5.71 0 M 1.14 5.71 L 10.28 5.71}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}_{\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{b},j}}
incident to
\mathsf{b}
are of equal length, the lifts of
h
in
\mathbb{H}
correspond to circles tangent to the horizontal axis that differ in radius by a factor
e^{2w_{\mathsf{b},j}}
.
In particular, the lifts of
h
at the endpoints of two lifts of a rib related by
z\mapsto e^{L_{i}}z
differ in radius by a factor

\displaystyle\exp\left(2\sum_{j=1}^{\deg(\mathsf{b})}w_{\mathsf{b},j}\right)=e^{L_{\mathsf{b}}}

as well.
Combining the last two relations proves (
37
).

Figure 12:
The reconstruction of Figure
11
with the help of the boundary parameters
(w_{\mathsf{b},j})_{j=1}^{\deg(\mathsf{b})}
and
(v_{\mathsf{b},j})_{j=1}^{\operatorname{nonid}(\mathsf{b})}
.

Knowledge of
(w_{\mathsf{b},j})_{j=1}^{\deg(\mathsf{b})}
and
(v_{\mathsf{b},j})_{j=1}^{\operatorname{nonid}(\mathsf{b})}
is sufficient to reconstruct the picture in
\mathbb{H}
up to scaling (see Figure
12
): choosing the first rib
\rho
as a unit-radius semicircle, the radii and positions of the other (lifts of) ribs are uniquely determined by the log-ratios of the radii and the tangency requirements.
The unique hyperbolic generator that fixes
\infty
and maps
\rho
to its sibling
\rho^{\prime}
of radius
e^{L_{i}}
also fixes a unique vertical geodesic that intersects all ribs.
Translating all ribs horizontally to align this geodesic with the vertical axis, we reproduce the original representation of the neighbourhood of
\mathsf{b}
in
\mathbb{H}
up to rescaling.
In particular, it unambiguously fixes the gluing prescription of the quadrilaterals and wedges.
∎

The situation in the case
L_{\mathsf{b}}=0
is slightly different.
As already observed in Lemma
9
, all corners of
\mathsf{b}
in this case are ideal so the cusp region consists of
\deg(\mathsf{b})
hyperbolic quadrilaterals only.
Considering the unit-length horocycle around
\mathsf{b}
, it is then natural to define
w_{\mathsf{b},j}
to be the length of horocyclic segment contained in

.

Lemma 13
.

If
\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})
corresponds to a cusp,
L_{\mathsf{b}}=0
, then these labels satisfy

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\deg(\mathsf{b})}w_{\mathsf{b},j}=1

(38)

and they uniquely determine the gluing of the quadrilaterals
\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to7.4pt{\vbox to9.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-0.42513pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope

incident to
\mathsf{b}
.

Proof.

Let us consider the neighbourhood of
\mathsf{b}
in
\mathbb{H}
with the associated parabolic generator
z\mapsto z+1
so that the cusp is located at
\infty
, see Figure
13
.
In this case the unit-length horocycle is located at height one and the parameter
w_{\mathsf{b},j}
measures the width of the vertical strip corresponding to

.
Since this clearly amounts to a partition of the unit-interval, (

) follows.
Moreover, the picture and therefore the gluing prescription of the quadrilaterals is fully characterized by
(w_{\mathsf{b},j})_{j=1}^{\deg(\mathsf{b})}
.

Example of the cusp region in
\mathbb{H}
for a vertex
\mathsf{b}
with
L_{\mathsf{b}}=0
and
\deg(\mathsf{b})=3
.

∎

We are now ready to properly define the mapping
\mathsf{Spine}
of Theorem
7
.
If
\mathsf{X}\in\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}(0,\mathbf{L})
, then by Lemma
9
we have that the tree structure of the spine gives a bicolored tree
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}^{\mathrm{all}}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
.
Lemma
11
, Lemma
12
and Lemma
13
together imply that

\displaystyle\lambda\coloneqq\left((\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}))_{\vec{\mathsf{e}}\in\vec{\mathsf{E}}(\mathfrak{t})},\left((w_{\mathsf{b},j})_{j=1}^{\deg(\mathsf{b})},(v_{\mathsf{b},j})_{j=1}^{\operatorname{nonid}(\mathsf{b})}\right)_{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})}\right)\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L}).

(39)

Moreover, each boundary rib is incident to a boundary vertex so, according to Lemma
12
and Lemma
13
, the gluing of the neighbouring quadrilaterals or quadrilateral-wedge pairs is uniquely determined.
We thus conclude the following.

Proposition 14
.

For
n\geq 2
and
\mathbf{L}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n}
, associating to
\mathsf{X}\in\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}(0,\mathbf{L})
the pair
\mathsf{Spine}(\mathsf{X})=(\mathfrak{t},\lambda)
consisting of the tree
\mathfrak{t}
and the point (
39
) in the polytope
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})
determines a well-defined injective mapping
\mathsf{Spine}:\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}(0,\mathbf{L})\to\bigsqcup_{\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}^{\mathrm{all}}_{n}(\mathbf{L})}\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})
.

2.5
Bijection proof

With our description of the mapping
\mathsf{Spine}
in place, it is straightforward to formulate the inverse construction.
Given a
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}^{\mathrm{all}}(\mathbf{L})
and a point
\lambda\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})
, let us construct a hyperbolic surface
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{Glue}(\mathfrak{t},\lambda)
.
To this end, we introduce the parameters associated to
\lambda
as in (
39
) and associate to each edge
\mathsf{e}\in\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})
the unique quadrilateral
\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to7.4pt{\vbox to9.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-0.42513pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

composed of congruent triangles with angles

and to each non-ideal corner
\mathsf{c}\in\mathsf{C}(\mathfrak{t})
a wedge
\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to7.91pt{\vbox to8.62pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
