{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 8.86 C 2.95 4.92 3.44 5.91 4.92 0 M 4.92 0 C 6.4 5.91 6.89 4.92 9.84 8.86 M 9.84 8.86 C 4.92 10.83 4.92 10.83 0 8.86}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}_{\mathsf{c}}
.
The incidence relations of
\mathfrak{t}
prescribe which pairs of sides of these constituents should be glued to result in a rib of the surface.
As explained in the previous section, this gluing is unambiguous for the inner ribs, while the shearing for the boundary ribs is determined by the local structure around the boundary vertices
\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})
.
The result is a complete hyperbolic surface
\check{\mathsf{X}}
and its convex core (i.e. the surface obtained by amputating funnels at their closed geodesic) is a hyperbolic surface with a distinguished cusp and
n
labeled geodesic boundaries or cusps, see Figure
14
.

Figure 14:
An example of a tree
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}^{\mathrm{all}}(\mathbf{L})
with
n=4
and
L_{1},L_{2},L_{3}>0
,
L_{4}=0
, together with an illustration of the gluing prescription (bottom left) and the resulting surface
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{Glue}(\mathfrak{t},\lambda)\in\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}(\mathbf{L})
.

Lemma 15
.

This determines a well-defined mapping
\mathsf{Glue}:\bigsqcup_{\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}^{\mathrm{all}}_{n}(\mathbf{L})}\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}\to\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}(0,\mathbf{L})
.

Proof.

Because of the planarity of the gluing prescription,
\check{\mathsf{X}}
is clearly a hyperbolic metric on an
(n+1)
-holed sphere.
We only need to check that the origin corresponds to a cusp and that the boundary with label
i
is a cusp if
L_{i}=0
or a funnel with the appropriate perimeter when
L_{i}>0
.
The latter conditions are granted by the constructions described in Lemma
12
and Lemma
13
because they apply to any choice of
(w_{\mathsf{b},j})_{j=1}^{\deg(\mathsf{b})}\in\Delta_{\deg(\mathsf{b})}
and
(v_{\mathsf{b},j})_{j=1}^{\operatorname{nonid}(\mathsf{b})}\in\Delta_{\operatorname{nonid}(\mathsf{b})}
.

In order for the origin to be a cusp, it is sufficient that there exists a matching choice of horocycles for all ideal vertices of the quadrilaterals and wedges that will be identified with the origin.
This can be achieved by selecting an arbitrary edge
\mathsf{e}\in\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})
and endowing the two ideal vertices of the quadrilateral
\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to7.4pt{\vbox to9.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-0.42513pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.14 5.71 C 2.85 6.28 5.14 8.56 5.71 11.42 M 1.14 5.71 C 2.85 5.14 5.14 2.85 5.71 0 M 10.28 5.71 C 8.56 6.28 6.28 8.56 5.71 11.42 M 10.28 5.71 C 8.56 5.14 6.28 2.85 5.71 0 M 1.14 5.71 L 10.28 5.71}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}_{\mathsf{e}}
with an arbitrary symmetric choice of horocycles.
Then iteratively we can adjust the horocycles on the remaining quadrilaterals and wedges as follows.
For each boundary vertex
\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})
, a choice of symmetric horocycles on a single incident quadrilateral uniquely determines the remaining horocycles (see Lemma
12
), which will again be symmetric for each quadrilateral.
Similarly, for each inner vertex
\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})
the horocycles of one of the
\deg(v)
quadrilaterals determines uniquely a choice of symmetric horocycles for all them.
Since
\mathfrak{t}
has the structure of a tree, the iteration determines unique matching horocycles everywhere.
∎

To prove Theorem
7
it remains to show that
\mathsf{Glue}
and
\mathsf{Spine}
are inverses of each other.

Proof of Theorem
7
.

That
\mathsf{Glue}\circ\mathsf{Spine}(X)=X
for every
\mathsf{X}\in\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}(0,\mathbf{L})
follows directly from the construction of
\mathsf{Glue}
and our discussion in the last subsection.
It remains to verify that for
\mathsf{X}=\mathsf{Glue}(\mathfrak{t},\lambda)
the spine construction gives
\mathsf{Spine}(X)=(\mathfrak{t},\lambda)
again.
In particular, we need to convince ourselves that the subset
\Sigma\subset\check{\mathsf{X}}
given by the union of diagonals of the quadrilaterals
\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to7.4pt{\vbox to9.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-0.42513pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.14 5.71 C 2.85 6.28 5.14 8.56 5.71 11.42 M 1.14 5.71 C 2.85 5.14 5.14 2.85 5.71 0 M 10.28 5.71 C 8.56 6.28 6.28 8.56 5.71 11.42 M 10.28 5.71 C 8.56 5.14 6.28 2.85 5.71 0 M 1.14 5.71 L 10.28 5.71}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}_{\mathsf{e}}
for
\mathsf{e}\in\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})
together with the points corresponding to the inner vertices is the cut-locus of
\check{\mathsf{X}}
.

By the gluing prescription,
\check{\mathsf{X}}\setminus\Sigma
has convex boundary and is topologically a punctured disk.
Therefore, every point
x\in\check{\mathsf{X}}\setminus\Sigma
admits a unique geodesic to the origin contained in
\check{\mathsf{X}}\setminus\Sigma
.
Moreover, for every
x\in\Sigma
there are a multiple such geodesics that are contained in
\check{\mathsf{X}}\setminus\Sigma
except for their endpoint and all of them have the same length.
Let now
x\in\check{\mathsf{X}}
and
\gamma
be a geodesic from
x
to the origin that intersects
\Sigma
away from its starting point.
Then letting
y\in\Sigma
be the last such intersection, we know that the final segment of
\gamma
is a geodesic from
y
to the origin that is contained in
\check{\mathsf{X}}\setminus\Sigma
.
We can thus find another path from
x
to the origin of the same length by replacing this final segment by one of the other such geodesics starting at
y
.
But by construction this path is not geodesic because of the bend at
y
, showing that
\gamma
is not of minimal length.
This proves that the geodesics to the origin in
\check{\mathsf{X}}\setminus\Sigma
are length minimizing, and therefore that
\Sigma
is the cut-locus.

Hence, the combinatorial tree identified in the spine construction
\mathsf{Spine}(X)
is precisely
\mathfrak{t}
, the quadrilaterals
(\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to7.4pt{\vbox to9.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-0.42513pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

and wedges
(\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to7.91pt{\vbox to8.62pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

are exactly the ones appearing in the partition of
\check{\mathsf{X}}
described in Lemma

.
Since the tree labels are intrinsic to the geometry of the quadrilaterals or the funnel/cusp region, they clearly match
\lambda
.
∎

3
Weil–Petersson Poisson structure and measure

We start with some background on the Weil-Petersson structures on Teichmüller space.
Let
S
be a fixed surface of genus
0
with
n+1
boundaries labeled
0,\ldots,n
.
For
\mathbf{L}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}_{\geq 0}
, the
Teichmüller space

\mathcal{T}_{0,n+1}(\mathbf{L})
consists of pairs
(\mathsf{X},f)
where
\mathsf{X}
is a hyperbolic surface with geodesic boundaries and/or cusps and where
f:S\to X
is an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism such that for each
i=0,\ldots,n
,
f
shrinks the
i
th boundary to a cusp if
L_{i}=0
or maps the boundary to a geodesic boundary of
\mathsf{X}
of length
L_{i}>0
.
Two pairs
(\mathsf{X},f)
and
(\mathsf{Y},g)
are regarded equivalent, if there exists an isometry
h:\mathsf{X}\to\mathsf{Y}
such that
h\circ f:S\to\mathsf{Y}
is isotopic to
g:S\to\mathsf{Y}
(allowing boundary reparametrization).

The Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}_{0,n+1}(\mathbf{L})
comes with a canonical symplectic structure given by the Weil-Petersson 2-form
\omega_{\mathrm{WP}}
.
It takes on a particularly simple form
[
58
]
in Fenchel-Nielsen coordinates associated to a pants decomposition of
S
,

\displaystyle\omega_{\mathrm{WP}}=\sum_{\gamma}\mathrm{d}\ell_{\gamma}\wedge\mathrm{d}\tau_{\gamma}

(40)

involving the lengths
\ell_{\gamma}
and twists
\tau_{\gamma}
associated to the closed geodesics
\gamma
of the pants decomposition.
The associated Weil-Petersson Poisson structure is determined by the bivector

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{WP}}=\sum_{\gamma}\frac{\partial}{\partial\ell_{\gamma}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau_{\gamma}},

(41)

Both the symplectic and the Poisson structure are mapping class group invariant, and therefore descend to moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}(\mathbf{L})
.
However, these expressions are not very useful for our purposes because the Fenchel-Nielsen coordinates are not easily related to the tree decorations.

In
[
13
]
it was observed that the Penner coordinates
[
48
]
on
\mathcal{T}_{0,1+n}(\mathbf{0})
, associated to carefully chosen triangulations of the cusped surfaces, are adapted well to the tree decoration.
Although this coordinate system does not generalize easily to the case of geodesic boundaries, the closely related
shear coordinates
do.
As we will explain in the next sections, it is more convenient to work with the Weil-Petersson Poisson structure than with the symplectic structure, because the former has a simple expression in terms of shears on Teichmüller space and in terms of the tree decoration
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})
.

3.1
Thurston–Fock shear coordinates

A triangulation
\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to12.18pt{\vbox to11.04pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower 0.16907pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

of

is a maximal set of disjoint arcs starting and ending on the boundaries such that each connected component of the complement is a hexagon with three sides corresponding to arcs interleaved with three sides corresponding to segments of the boundary, see the left-hand side of Figure

for an example.

and a hyperbolic surface

, each arc

{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}

{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0.79 C 6.3 6.3 6.3 9.45 7.87 14.96 M 7.87 14.96 C 9.45 9.45 9.45 6.3 15.75 0.79 M 15.75 0.79 C 9.45 3.15 6.3 3.15 0 0.79}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}
determines an arc
f(\alpha)
on
\mathsf{X}
starting and ending on a geodesic boundary or cusp.
The homotopy class of such arcs, where one allows sliding the endpoints along the boundaries, contains a unique geodesic representative
\gamma^{\perp}_{\alpha}
that meets the boundaries perpendicularly at the endpoints (or runs into the cusp).
This gives a canonical tiling of
\mathsf{X}
by right-angled hyperbolic hexagons (or hyperbolic
k
-gons with
6-k
ideal vertices and
2k-6
right angles, for
k=3,4,5
, depending on the number of adjacent cusps).
But one can also canonically associate to
\alpha
a geodesic
\gamma^{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha}
on
\mathsf{X}
that spirals into the boundaries at the endpoints of
\alpha
in a clockwise fashion (of course no spiralling happens when it ends at a cusp).
Informally, we think of
\gamma^{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha}
as the limiting geodesic obtained from
\gamma^{\perp}_{\alpha}
by moving both endpoints clockwise along the boundary at constant rate, see the right-hand side of Figure
15
.
The result is a tiling of
\mathsf{X}
by
2n-2
ideal triangles, see Figure
16
for the simple case of
n=2
corresponding to a pair of pants.

Figure 16:
Left: example surface
\mathsf{X}
in the case
n=2
, corresponding to a pair of pants, with the perpendicular geodesics
\gamma_{\alpha}^{\perp}
of a triangulation in black and the spiralling geodesics
\gamma_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{S}}
in purple. Middle: the corresponding fundamental domains for
\mathsf{X}
in
\mathbb{H}
consisting either of a pair of right-angled hexagons (black) or a pair of ideal triangles (purple). Right: the definition of the shear coordinate as a signed hyperbolic distance along
\alpha
. In this example
z_{\alpha}<0
.

This ideal triangulation gives rise to the Thurston-Fock shear coordinates on
\mathcal{T}_{0,n+1}(\mathbf{L})
, first introduced by Thurston
[
56
]
and generalized by Fock
[
28
]
.
See
[
50
, Section 2.4]
for a recent overview.
The shear
z_{\alpha}
associated to the arc
\alpha\in\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to12.18pt{\vbox to11.04pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower 0.16907pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0.79 C 6.3 6.3 6.3 9.45 7.87 14.96 M 7.87 14.96 C 9.45 9.45 9.45 6.3 15.75 0.79 M 15.75 0.79 C 9.45 3.15 6.3 3.15 0 0.79}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}
can be defined as follows.
The geodesic
\gamma^{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha}
on
\mathsf{X}
is adjacent to a pair of ideal triangles, see right-hand side of Figure
16
.
If
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
are the positions of the ideal vertices of these triangles lifted to
\mathbb{H}
, such that
\alpha
points from
x_{1}
to
x_{2}
with
x_{3}
on the right and
x_{4}
on the left, then the shear is the logarithmic cross ratio

\displaystyle z_{\alpha}=\log\frac{(x_{3}-x_{1})(x_{4}-x_{2})}{(x_{3}-x_{2})(x_{1}-x_{4})}.

(42)

Equivalently,
z_{\alpha}
can be understood as the signed hyperbolic distance along
\gamma^{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha}
between the orthogonal projections onto
\gamma^{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha}
of the opposite vertices (corresponding to
x_{3}
and
x_{4}
).
By
[
50
, Theorem 4.4]
, the mapping
(\mathsf{X},f)\mapsto(z_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to12.18pt{\vbox to11.04pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower 0.16907pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}

is a real-analytic homeomorphism

\displaystyle\mathsf{Shear}:\mathcal{T}_{0,n+1}(\mathbf{L})\to\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to12.18pt{\vbox to11.04pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower 0.16907pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

that start at the

th boundary (counting twice the arcs that start and end there).
Since the shears obey

independent constraints,

has dimension
2n-4
.

We have seen that the Weil-Petersson symplectic form is easily expressed in Fenchel-Nielsen coordinates.
In the case of shear coordinates, however, it is the dual Weil-Petersson Poisson structure that admits a simple expression.
Fock introduced
[
29
]
(see also
[
50
, Definition 4.9]
) the bivector
6
6

6

Note that the factor of
\frac{1}{2}
does not appear in
[
29
]
or
[
50
]
. Several different conventions are used in the literature for the normalization of the Weil-Petersson symplectic or Poisson structure. Our choice is the one compatible with the symplectic structure
\omega_{\mathrm{WP}}=-2\sum_{\text{triangles}}\left(\mathrm{d}\log\lambda_{\alpha}\wedge\mathrm{d}\log\lambda_{\beta}+\cdots\right)
in Penner’s lambda-lengths in the case of punctured surfaces, as well as the Weil-Petersson volume computations of Mirzakhani
[
42
]
. Note for instance that
[
49
, Theorem 3.3.6]
and
[
19
, Equation (2.8)]
differ by a factor of
2
.

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to12.18pt{\vbox to11.04pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower 0.16907pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0.79 C 6.3 6.3 6.3 9.45 7.87 14.96 M 7.87 14.96 C 9.45 9.45 9.45 6.3 15.75 0.79 M 15.75 0.79 C 9.45 3.15 6.3 3.15 0 0.79}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\text{triangles}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{\alpha}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{\beta}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{\beta}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{\gamma}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{\gamma}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{\alpha}}\right)

(44)

on

, where the sum runs over the triangles of

and the bounding arcs of each triangle are

,

It was later
[
30
,
18
]
demonstrated to agree with the Weil-Petersson Poisson structure
\pi_{\mathrm{WP}}
from (

).

3.2
Relation to the spine construction

From here on we restrict to the case
L_{0}=0
and
\mathbf{L}=(L_{1},\ldots,L_{n})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n}
.
We now explain how to associate to any hyperbolic surface
(\mathsf{X},f)\in\mathcal{T}_{0,n+1}(0,\mathbf{L})
a canonical triangulation of
S
, in a similar spirit to
[
13
, Section 3]
but using the generalized spine and shear coordinates instead of Penner coordinates.

Recall the construction of the spine
\Sigma\subset\check{\mathsf{X}}
of Lemma
8
and the associated partition of
\check{\mathsf{X}}
into quadrangles and wedge of Lemma
10
.
For each edge
\mathsf{e}\in\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})
we take an arc in the quadrangle

43
) thus gives an injective mapping

\displaystyle\mathsf{Shear}_{\mathfrak{t}}:\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})\to\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to12.18pt{\vbox to11.04pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower 0.16907pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0.79 C 6.3 6.3 6.3 9.45 7.87 14.96 M 7.87 14.96 C 9.45 9.45 9.45 6.3 15.75 0.79 M 15.75 0.79 C 9.45 3.15 6.3 3.15 0 0.79}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}_{\mathfrak{t}}}(0,\mathbf{L})\subset\mathbb{R}^{3n-3}.

(46)

Thanks to (
45
), when viewing the shears as indexed by the edges of the combinatorial triangulation, this mapping is independent of the choice of representative
(\mathsf{X},f)
.

dual to
\mathsf{e}
is adjacent to an ideal triangle (in purple) with a vertex at
\infty
. The half-shear
z_{\vec{\mathsf{e}}}
is equal to
w_{\mathsf{b},j}
.

Let us identify the mapping
\mathsf{Shear}_{\mathfrak{t}}
explicitly in terms of the coordinates of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})
.
For this it is convenient to introduce the
half-shear

z_{\vec{\mathsf{e}}}
associated to an orientation of an edge
\mathsf{e}\in\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})
of the spine.
If
\alpha
is the geodesic arc associated to the quadrangle
\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to7.4pt{\vbox to9.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-0.42513pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

, then

comes with a canonical marking at its midpoint, measured with respect to the horocycle at the origin.
In case
\alpha
and
\mathsf{e}
intersect, this corresponds precisely to their intersection point (see left side of Figure

).
We consider the ideal triangle adjacent to
\alpha
on the side corresponding to starting point of
\vec{\mathsf{e}}
.
Then the half-shear
z_{\vec{\mathsf{e}}}
is the signed hyperbolic distance along
\alpha
between its midpoint and the orthogonal projection onto
\alpha
of the opposite vertex of this ideal triangle.
It should be clear that then the shear
z_{\mathsf{e}}
of
\alpha
equals the sum of the half-shears of the orientations of
\mathsf{e}
,

\displaystyle z_{\mathsf{e}}=z_{\vec{\mathsf{e}}}+z_{\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}}.

(47)

The expression for
z_{\vec{\mathsf{e}}}
depends on the type of vertex at which
\vec{\mathsf{e}}
starts.

starts at a boundary vertex

.

correspond to semicircles whose radii differ by a factor

,
the horocycle

at the origin lifts to a pair of horocycles that differ in size by a hyperbolic distance

.
It follows directly that the midpoint of

is at (signed) distance

\displaystyle z_{\vec{\mathsf{e}}}=w_{\mathsf{b},j}

(48)

from the top.

starts at a boundary vertex

corresponding to a cusp,

, then the corresponding ideal triangle in

aligns with the boundary ribs of
\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to7.4pt{\vbox to9.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-0.42513pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

.
Due to symmetry (see Figure

) the half-shear
z_{\vec{\mathsf{e}}}=0
vanishes in this case.

Figure 18:
Here we use the abbreviated notation
\varphi_{j}=\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},j})
,
j=1,2,3
.

If
\vec{\mathsf{e}}
starts at an inner vertex
\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})
of degree
3
, let us assume for concreteness that
\vec{\mathsf{e}}=\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},1}
and examine the angles
\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},j})
,
j=1,2,3
, around
\mathsf{v}
.
If we draw the arc dual to
\vec{\mathsf{e}}
vertically in
\mathbb{H}
, then the ideal triangle dual to
\mathsf{v}
looks like the one in Figure
18
.
Elementary trigonometry shows that
\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},j})
,
j=1,2,3
are precisely the angles of the Euclidean triangle formed by
\mathsf{v}
and the two ideal vertices on the horizontal axis.
In particular,
e^{z_{\vec{\mathsf{e}}}}
is the ratio of lengths of the right side to the bottom side.
Hence, the sine law tells us that

\displaystyle z_{\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},1}}=\log\frac{\sin\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},2})}{\sin\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},3})}.

(49)

Of course, this formula holds more generally under cyclic permutation of the indices
1,2,3
.

Figure 19:
To compute the shear associated to an arc
\alpha
that spirals into a boundary, we examine the construction of Figure
12
.
The lengths of the segments on the horizontal axis obey (
50
).

For each corner
\mathsf{c}
of a boundary vertex
\mathsf{b}
we have a geodesic arc
\alpha
spiralling into the boundary associated to
\mathsf{b}
.
Assuming
\mathsf{c}
is non-ideal, the construction of Lemma
12
and Figure
12
allows us to establish the geometry of the two neighbouring ideal triangles, see Figure
19
.
The hyperbolic distances
z_{\mathsf{c}}
,
w_{\mathsf{b},j}
,
v_{\mathsf{b},j}
and
w_{\mathsf{b},j+1}
can easily be expressed in terms of the segment lengths
a,b,c,d
indicated in the figure as

\displaystyle e^{z_{\mathsf{c}}}=\frac{b}{a},\quad e^{w_{\mathsf{b},j}}=\frac{b+c}{c},\quad e^{v_{\mathsf{b},j}}=\frac{b+c+d}{b+c},\quad e^{w_{\mathsf{b},j+1}}=\frac{a+b+c+d}{b+c+d}.

(50)

Solving for
z_{\mathsf{c}}
gives

\displaystyle z_{\mathsf{c}}=-v_{\mathsf{b},j}+\log\frac{1-e^{-w_{\mathsf{b},j}}}{e^{w_{\mathsf{b},j+1}}-1}.

(51)

This formula remains valid if
\mathsf{c}
is ideal as long as we admit that
v_{\mathsf{b},j}=0
by convention.
In case
L_{\mathsf{b}}=0
, we can refer to Figure
13
to see that
z_{\mathsf{c}}=\log(w_{\mathsf{b},j}/w_{\mathsf{b},j+1})
.

Lemma 16
.

\mathsf{Shear}_{\mathfrak{t}}
is real-analytic for each
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}^{\mathrm{all}}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
. Its image is of full dimension in
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to12.18pt{\vbox to11.04pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower 0.16907pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0.79 C 6.3 6.3 6.3 9.45 7.87 14.96 M 7.87 14.96 C 9.45 9.45 9.45 6.3 15.75 0.79 M 15.75 0.79 C 9.45 3.15 6.3 3.15 0 0.79}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}_{\mathfrak{t}}}(0,\mathbf{L})
if and only if
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
.

Proof.

Assuming all inner vertices are of degree
3
, we have provided explicit formulas above for the shear coordinates comprising
\mathsf{Shear}_{\mathfrak{t}}
, which are analytic by inspection.
In case of an inner vertex
\mathsf{v}
of degree
k>3
, the associated geodesic arcs form a triangulated ideal
k
-gon.
It is straightforward to check that the shears of the diagonals and the half-shears of the sides of this
k
-gon are analytic functions of the
k
angles
\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},j})
,
j=1,\ldots,k
.
We omit the details, because we won’t need the formulas in the following.

Recall that
\dim\mathcal{Z}(0,\mathbf{L})=2n-4
.
Comparing with the dimension of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})
given in (
32
), we see that
\dim\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to12.18pt{\vbox to11.04pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower 0.16907pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0.79 C 6.3 6.3 6.3 9.45 7.87 14.96 M 7.87 14.96 C 9.45 9.45 9.45 6.3 15.75 0.79 M 15.75 0.79 C 9.45 3.15 6.3 3.15 0 0.79}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}_{\mathfrak{t}}}(0,\mathbf{L})=\dim\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})
if and only if
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
.
Together with injectivity and analyticity, this verifies the claim.
∎

Proposition 17
.

The subset
\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}^{\circ}(0,\mathbf{L})\coloneqq\mathsf{Glue}(\bigsqcup_{\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}(\mathbf{L})}\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L}))\subset\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}(0,\mathbf{L})
is of full Weil-Petersson measure.

Proof.

It follows from the previous lemma, that the complement
\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}(0,\mathbf{L})\setminus\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}^{\circ}(0,\mathbf{L})
is covered by finitely many real-analytic hypersurfaces in
\mathcal{T}_{0,n+1}(0,\mathbf{L})
.
These have vanishing Weil-Petersson measure.
∎

From here on we will restrict to
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
.
This means that all inner vertices are of degree
3
and all corners of those boundary vertices for which
L_{i}>0
are non-ideal.
In this case we choose the ordering of edges and corners around a boundary vertex
\mathsf{b}
such that for
j=1,\ldots,\deg(\mathsf{b})
the corner
\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{b},j}
sits between the edges
\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{b},j}
and
\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{b},j+1}
(where we use the convention
w_{\mathsf{b},\deg(\mathsf{b})+1}=w_{\mathsf{b},1}
).

Figure 20:
The tree
\mathfrak{t}
gives rise to a canonical ideal triangulation of
\mathsf{X}
that is illustrated on the right, where edges of the triangulation that are dual to edges of
\mathfrak{t}
are colored in red.

3.3
Weil–Petersson Poisson structure on the tree decoration

For
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
we introduce the bivector
\pi_{\mathfrak{t}}\in\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{R}^{6n-6}
as

\pi_{\mathfrak{t}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},j})}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},j+1})}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})}\sum_{j=1}^{\deg(\mathsf{b})}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{\mathsf{b},j}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{\mathsf{b},j}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{\mathsf{b},j}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{\mathsf{b},j+1}}\right),

(52)

where it is understood that
\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},4}=\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},1}
and
w_{\mathsf{b},\deg(\mathsf{b})+1}=w_{\mathsf{b},1}
.
We may equally think of
\pi_{\mathfrak{t}}\in\bigwedge^{2}T\mathbb{R}^{6n-6}
as a constant bivector field on the manifold
\mathbb{R}^{6n-6}
.

Lemma 18
.

For each
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
, the bivector
\pi_{\mathfrak{t}}
defines a Poisson structure
\{f,g\}_{\mathfrak{t}}=\pi_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathrm{d}f,\mathrm{d}g)
on
\mathbb{R}^{6n-6}
with symplectic leaves of dimension
2n-4
. The polytope
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})
of (
31
) is (an open subset of) such a symplectic leaf and thus comes with a corresponding symplectic form
\omega_{\mathfrak{t}}
.

Proof.

The bracket
\{f,g\}_{\mathfrak{t}}
of a constant bivector field automatically satisfies the Jacobi identity and therefore determines a Poisson structure.
It clearly satisfies

\displaystyle\forall_{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})}\forall_{1\leq j\leq\deg(\mathsf{b})}\,\left\{\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{b},j}),\,\cdot\,\right\}_{\mathfrak{t}}

\displaystyle=0,\quad

\displaystyle\forall_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})}\,\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{3}\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},j}),\,\cdot\,\right\}_{\mathfrak{t}}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\forall_{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})}\,\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{\deg(\mathsf{b})}v_{\mathsf{b},j},\,\cdot\,\right\}_{\mathfrak{t}}

\displaystyle=0,\quad

\displaystyle\forall_{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})}\,\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{\deg(\mathsf{b})}w_{\mathsf{b},j},\,\cdot\,\right\}_{\mathfrak{t}}

\displaystyle=0,

and it is easily checked that these span the kernel of
\pi_{\mathfrak{t}}
(seen as a mapping
\pi_{\mathfrak{t}}^{\sharp}:T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{6n-6}\to T\mathbb{R}^{6n-6}
).
From the definition (
31
), we observe that
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})
corresponds to the non-empty open subset
\{\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}})+\varphi(\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu})<\pi\text{ for }\mathsf{e}\in\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t}),\,v_{\mathsf{b},j},w_{\mathsf{b},j}>0\text{ for }\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t}),j=1,\ldots,\deg(\mathsf{b})\}
of a symplectic leaf (namely a translation of the image of
\pi_{\mathfrak{t}}^{\sharp}
).
The corresponding symplectic form on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})
is related to
\pi_{\mathfrak{t}}
via
\omega_{\mathfrak{t}}(\pi_{\mathfrak{t}}^{\sharp}\alpha,\pi_{\mathfrak{t}}^{\sharp}\beta)=-\pi_{\mathfrak{t}}(\alpha,\beta)
, see e.g.
[
21
, Prop. 2.24]
.
∎

We are primarily interested in the volume form on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})
induced by its symplectic structure.
Luckily, it can be computed directly from the bivector
\pi_{\mathfrak{t}}
.

Lemma 19
.

Up to an irrelevant sign the Liouville volume form
\mu_{\mathfrak{t}}=\pm\omega_{\mathfrak{t}}^{n-2}/(n-2)!
on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})
is equal to

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathfrak{t}}=2^{n-2}\bigwedge_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})}\left(\mathrm{d}\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},1})\wedge\mathrm{d}\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},2})\right)\wedge\bigwedge_{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})}\bigwedge_{j=1}^{\deg(\mathsf{b})-1}\left(\mathrm{d}v_{\mathsf{b},j}\wedge\mathrm{d}w_{\mathsf{b},j}\right).

Proof.

Clearly
(\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},1}),\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},2}))_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})},((v_{\mathsf{b},j},w_{\mathsf{b},j})_{j=1}^{\deg(\mathsf{b})-1})_{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})}
constitutes a proper coordinate chart on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})
.
In these coordinates, the non-degenerate Poisson bivector on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})
is

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathfrak{t}}|_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})}\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},1})}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},2})}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})}\sum_{j=1}^{\deg(\mathsf{b})-1}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{\mathsf{b},j}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{\mathsf{b},j}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{\mathsf{b},j}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{\mathsf{b},j+1}}\right).

From the simple block structure we see that the top exterior power
(\pi_{\mathfrak{t}}|_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})})^{n-2}/(n-2)!\in\bigwedge^{2n-4}T\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})
is simply

\displaystyle\frac{(\pi_{\mathfrak{t}}|_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})})^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}=2^{2-n}\bigwedge_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},1})}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},2})}\right)\wedge\bigwedge_{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})}\bigwedge_{j=1}^{\deg(\mathsf{b})-1}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{\mathsf{b},j}}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{\mathsf{b},j}}\right).

Since
\pi_{\mathfrak{t}}
and
\omega_{\mathfrak{t}}
are constant in these coordinates, the natural pairing of
\bigwedge^{2n-4}T\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})
and
\bigwedge^{2n-4}T^{*}\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})
shows that
\mu_{\mathfrak{t}}
is
2^{n-2}
times the standard
(2n-4)
-dimensional volume form in these coordinates.
∎

Proposition 20
.

For each
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
, the mapping
\mathsf{Glue}|_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})}
is a Poisson isomorphism onto its image
\mathsf{Glue}(\mathfrak{t},\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L}))
equipped with the Weil-Petersson Poisson structure.

Proof.

It suffices to compute the Poisson brackets
\{\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\}_{\mathfrak{t}}
of the components of
\mathsf{Shear}_{\mathfrak{t}}
and check that they match with the bracket
\{\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\}_{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to12.18pt{\vbox to11.04pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower 0.16907pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}

associated to (

) in Thurston-Fock coordinates.
Since (

) is local to the vertices of
\mathfrak{t}
, it is clear that

(53)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\cot\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},1})\cot\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},2})+\cot\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},2})\cot\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},3})+\cot\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},3})\cot\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},1})\right)

(54)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}=\{z_{\mathsf{e}},z_{\mathsf{e}^{\prime}}\}_{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to12.18pt{\vbox to11.04pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower 0.16907pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

) is due to a version of Hermite’s cotangent identity, since

.

.
