{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0.79 C 6.3 6.3 6.3 9.45 7.87 14.96 M 7.87 14.96 C 9.45 9.45 9.45 6.3 15.75 0.79 M 15.75 0.79 C 9.45 3.15 6.3 3.15 0 0.79}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}}.

Finally, if
\mathsf{c},\mathsf{c}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{C}(\mathfrak{t})
are two corners, then clearly
\{z_{\mathsf{c}},z_{\mathsf{c}^{\prime}}\}_{\mathfrak{t}}=0
unless
\mathsf{c}
and
\mathsf{c}^{\prime}
are adjacent at the same boundary vertex
\mathsf{b}
.
If they correspond to
\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{b},j},\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{b},j+1}
, and
\deg(\mathsf{b})>2
, then

\displaystyle\{z_{\mathsf{c}},z_{\mathsf{c}^{\prime}}\}_{\mathfrak{t}}

\displaystyle=\left\{-v_{\mathsf{b},j}+\log\frac{1-e^{-w_{\mathsf{b},j}}}{e^{w_{\mathsf{b},j+1}}-1},-v_{\mathsf{b},j+1}+\log\frac{1-e^{-w_{\mathsf{b},j+1}}}{e^{w_{\mathsf{b},j+2}}-1}\right\}_{\mathfrak{t}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{e^{w_{\mathsf{b},j+1}}-1}\{v_{\mathsf{b},j},w_{\mathsf{b},j+1}\}_{\mathfrak{t}}+\frac{e^{w_{\mathsf{b},j+1}}}{e^{w_{\mathsf{b},j+1}}-1}\{w_{\mathsf{b},j+1},v_{\mathsf{b},j+1}\}_{\mathfrak{t}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}=\{z_{\mathsf{c}},z_{\mathsf{c}^{\prime}}\}_{\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to12.18pt{\vbox to11.04pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower 0.16907pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0.79 C 6.3 6.3 6.3 9.45 7.87 14.96 M 7.87 14.96 C 9.45 9.45 9.45 6.3 15.75 0.79 M 15.75 0.79 C 9.45 3.15 6.3 3.15 0 0.79}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}}.

If
\deg(\mathsf{b})=2
then both Poisson brackets vanish due to the constraint
z_{\mathsf{c}}+z_{\mathsf{c}^{\prime}}=L_{\mathsf{b}}/2
.
This finishes the proof.
∎

Finally we have all the ingredients for our main theorem.

Proof of Theorem
1
.

Proposition
17
gives the existence of the subset
\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}^{\circ}(0,\mathbf{L})\subset\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}(0,\mathbf{L})
of full measure and Theorem
7
provides the bijection restricted to
\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}^{\circ}(0,\mathbf{L})
.
It is a Poisson isomorphism by Proposition
20
, so the Weil-Petersson volume form on
\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}^{\circ}(0,\mathbf{L})
agrees with the pull-back along
\mathsf{Spine}
of the Liouville volume form on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})
.
According to Lemma
19
the latter agrees with the (rescaled) Lebesgue measure (
5
).
∎

4
Computation of Weil–Petersson volumes

As an application of our tree bijection we turn to the computation of Weil-Petersson volumes.
After dealing with the case of fixed
n
we will turn attention to the generating functions.

4.1
Polytope volumes via inclusion-exclusion

As announced in the introduction, the main difficulty in computing the volumes
\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L}))
comes from the Delaunay condition
\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}})+\varphi(\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu})>\pi
, which is there to ensure that the edge
\mathsf{e}
of the spine has positive length.
In the volume computation of
[
13
, Section 4.3]
this was addressed, roughly speaking, by considering the generating function of volumes associated to rooted trees and introducing a “catalytic variable” to control the angle
\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}})
at the root.
Here we follow a complementary approach via inclusion-exclusion to reverse the Delaunay inequality.

If
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
and
\mathsf{A}\subset\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})
is a subset of the edges of
\mathfrak{t}
, that we call
anti-Delaunay edges
, then we introduce the new polytope

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{anti}}_{\mathfrak{t},\mathsf{A}}(\mathbf{L})\coloneqq\left\{\varphi:\vec{\mathsf{E}}(\mathfrak{t})\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\middle|\begin{array}[]{l}\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{b},i})=0\text{ for }\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t}),1\leq i\leq\deg(\mathsf{b}),\\
\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},i})>0\text{ for }\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t}),1\leq i\leq 3,\\
\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},1})+\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},2})+\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{v},3})=\pi\text{ for }\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t}),\\
\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}})+\varphi(\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vec{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{e}\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu})>\pi\text{ for }\mathsf{e}\in\mathsf{A}\end{array}\right\}\times\prod_{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\mathfrak{t})}\left(\Delta_{\deg(\mathsf{b})}(L_{\mathsf{b}}/2)\right)^{2}.

Note that it differs from
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})
only in the last condition on the angles, in particular there is no condition for the remaining edges
\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})\setminus\mathsf{A}
.
Observe also that an edge incident to a boundary vertex cannot obey the anti-Delaunay condition, so from here on we assume that the edges
\mathsf{A}
have their endpoints at the inner vertices
\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})
so that
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{anti}}_{\mathfrak{t},\mathsf{A}}(\mathbf{L})
is non-empty.
It is naturally equipped with same volume measure
\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{t}}
defined in (
5
).

Since the polytopes
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{anti}}_{\mathfrak{t},\mathsf{A}}(\mathbf{L})
have finite volume for all
\mathsf{A}
, an application of the inclusion-exclusion principle immediately yields the relation

\displaystyle\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L}))=\sum_{\mathsf{A}\subset\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})}(-1)^{|\mathsf{A}|}\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{anti}}_{\mathfrak{t},\mathsf{A}}(\mathbf{L})).

(60)

Recall from Section
1.1
the set
\widetilde{\mathfrak{T}}_{n}(\mathbf{L})\supset\mathfrak{T}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
of bicolored trees whose inner vertices are allowed to have degree
3
or larger.
Given
\mathfrak{t}
and
\mathsf{A}
, we define the
anti-Delaunay tree

\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{T}}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
to be the combinatorial tree obtained from
\mathfrak{t}
by contracting the edges
\mathsf{A}
.
To each inner vertex
\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})
of
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
one can associate a subtree
\mathfrak{t}_{\mathsf{v}}
of
\mathfrak{t}
including all edges of
\mathfrak{t}
incident to one of the vertices that was merged into
\mathsf{v}
.
For concreteness one may treat the leafs of
\mathfrak{t}_{\mathsf{v}}
as if they correspond to univalent boundary vertices, e.g. cusps.
See Figure
21
for an example.

Figure 21:
An example of a tree
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
with a choice
\mathsf{A}\subset\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})
of anti-Delaunay edges and the corresponding anti-Delaunay tree
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{T}}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
.

Lemma 21
.

The polytope
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t},\mathsf{A}}^{\mathrm{anti}}(\mathbf{L})
factors into a Cartesian product of polytopes associated to the (boundary and inner) vertices of the anti-Delaunay tree
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t},\mathsf{A}}^{\mathrm{anti}}(\mathbf{L})\cong\prod_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})}\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{v}}\prod_{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})}\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{b}}.

(61)

The volume factors correspondingly as

\displaystyle\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{anti}}_{\mathfrak{t},\mathsf{A}}(\mathbf{L}))=2^{n-2}\prod_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})}\frac{\pi^{2\deg(\mathsf{v})-4}}{(2\deg(\mathsf{v})-4)!}\prod_{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})}\frac{(L_{\mathsf{b}}/2)^{2\deg(\mathsf{b})-2}}{((\deg(\mathsf{b})-1)!)^{2}}.

(62)

In particular, it depends only on the degrees in
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
and not on the structure of the trees
(\mathfrak{t}_{\mathsf{v}})_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})}
.

Proof.

Since the only conditions in (
4.1
) that are not local to a vertex of
\mathfrak{t}
are the anti-Delaunay conditions for the edges
\mathsf{A}
, the factorization should be clear for the contracted tree
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
.
Since
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{b}}=\left(\Delta_{\deg(\mathsf{b})}(L_{\mathsf{b}}/2)\right)^{2}
is a product of two simplices, its Euclidean volume is

\displaystyle\left(\frac{(L_{\mathsf{b}}/2)^{\deg(\mathsf{b})-1}}{(\deg(\mathsf{b})-1)!}\right)^{2}.

For
\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})
, say of degree
\deg(\mathsf{v})=k+2
, we claim that
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{v}}\cong\Delta_{2k+1}^{(\pi)}
is also a simplex of dimension
2k-2
after a (volume-preserving) change of variables.
Note that
\mathfrak{t}_{\mathsf{v}}
has
k
inner vertices and
k+2
leaves.
Let us designate one of the leafs as the root and consider all oriented edge
\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{0},\ldots,\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{2k}
of
\mathfrak{t}_{\mathsf{v}}
that point away from the root, where by convention
\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{0}
starts at the root.
The anti-Delaunay condition turns into the requirement that
\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{i})>\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{j})+\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{k})
whenever
\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{j}
and
\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{k}
are the two children of
\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{i}
for
i>0
, keeping in mind the natural genealogical interpretation of the binary tree
\mathfrak{t}_{\mathsf{v}}
.
This makes sense for the root as well (
i=0
) if we use the convention
\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{0})=\pi
.
It motivates us to introduce
\widetilde{\varphi}(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{i})>0
for
i=0,\ldots,2k
to be the angle
\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{i})
minus the sum of the angles of its children, if any.
Then it is easy to see that
(\widetilde{\varphi}(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{i}))_{i=0}^{2k}\in\Delta_{2k+1}^{(\pi)}
, see Figure
22
.

Figure 22:
Example of a subtree
\mathfrak{t}_{\mathsf{v}}
for
\deg(\mathsf{v})=6
(so
k=4
) rooted at the bottom. The diagram on the right illustrates the partition
\sum_{i=0}^{2k}\widetilde{\varphi}(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{i})=\pi
.

The volume measure (without the power of
2
) on
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{v}}
is the Lebesgue measure in both sets of angles,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{1})\cdots\mathrm{d}\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{2k})=\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\varphi}(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{1})\cdots\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\varphi}(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{2k}).

Hence
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{v}}
contributes a volume
\pi^{2k}/(2k)!
.
Taking a product over all vertices of
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
and incorporating the conventional normalization
2^{n-2}
gives the claimed volume (
62
).
∎

It remains to sum over
\mathfrak{t}
and
\mathsf{A}
to obtain the Weil-Petersson volumes of Theorem
3
.

Proof of Theorem
3
.

Combining Corollary
2
and the inclusion-exclusion sum (
60
), we have

\displaystyle V_{0,1+n}(0,\mathbf{L})=\sum_{\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}(\mathbf{L})}\sum_{\mathsf{A}\subset\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})}(-1)^{|\mathsf{A}|}\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{anti}}_{\mathfrak{t},\mathsf{A}}(\mathbf{L})).

(63)

By the previous lemma the volume on the right-hand side depends only on the contracted tree
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
.
Counting the pairs
(\mathfrak{t},\mathsf{A})
resulting in the same such tree
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
is straightforward: for each
\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})
of degree
\deg(\mathsf{v})=k+2
the subtree
\mathfrak{t}_{\mathsf{v}}
can be any of the Catalan number
\binom{2k}{k}/(k+1)
of binary trees with
k
inner vertices.
Using also that
|\mathsf{A}|=\sum_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})}(\deg(\mathsf{v})-3)
, plugging in (
62
) leads to the expression

\displaystyle V_{0,1+n}(0,\mathbf{L})

\displaystyle=2^{n-2}\sum_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{T}}_{n}(\mathbf{L})}\prod_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})}\frac{(-1)^{\deg(\mathsf{v})-3}\pi^{2\deg(\mathsf{v})-4}}{(\deg(\mathsf{v})-1)!(\deg(\mathsf{v})-2)!}\prod_{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})}\frac{(L_{\mathsf{b}}/2)^{2\deg(\mathsf{b})-2}}{((\deg(\mathsf{b})-1)!)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{T}}_{n}(\mathbf{L})}\prod_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})}\frac{2^{\deg(\mathsf{v})-2}(-1)^{\deg(\mathsf{v})-3}\pi^{2\deg(\mathsf{v})-4}}{(\deg(\mathsf{v})-1)!(\deg(\mathsf{v})-2)!}\prod_{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})}\frac{2^{\deg(\mathsf{b})-1}(L_{\mathsf{b}}/2)^{2\deg(\mathsf{b})-2}}{((\deg(\mathsf{b})-1)!)^{2}},

where we used the combinatorial identity (
33
) in the second equality.
Recalling the definitions

\displaystyle\gamma_{k}=\frac{(-1)^{k}\pi^{2k-2}}{(k-1)!},\quad t_{k}(L)=\frac{2}{k!}\left(\frac{L}{2}\right)^{2k},

this corresponds precisely the claimed formula

\displaystyle V_{0,1+n}(0,\mathbf{L})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{T}}_{n}(\mathbf{L})}\prod_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})}\frac{2^{\deg(\mathsf{v})-2}}{(\deg(\mathsf{v})-1)!}\,\gamma_{\deg(\mathsf{v})-1}\prod_{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})}\frac{2^{\deg(\mathsf{b})-2}}{(\deg(\mathsf{b})-1)!}\,t_{\deg(\mathsf{b})-1}(L_{\mathsf{b}}).

∎

We emphasize that the relation with decorated anti-Delaunay trees involves inclusion-exclusion, and therefore we loose the strictly bijective correspondence.
Effectively we have related the Weil–Petersson measure on
\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}(0,\mathbf{L})
to a signed measure on

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}},\mathsf{A})}\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{anti}}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}},\mathsf{A}}(\mathbf{L}),

(64)

that is easier to work.
Besides volume computations, we can use it for integration against the Weil–Petersson measure as long as the integrand extends appropriately to trees with anti-Delaunay edges.
In Section
5
, we will see that this is the case for the distance statistic
D
.

4.2
The string equation

We start by introducing the generating functions of Weil-Petersson volumes a bit more carefully than we did in the introduction.
Following
[
15
]
, we introduce a weight
\mu\in\mathbb{R}[L^{2}]^{*}
as a linear functional on the ring of real even polynomials.
Then one may define the partition function of genus-
g
Weil–Petersson volumes as

\displaystyle F_{g}[\mu]=\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{\mu^{\otimes n}(V_{g,n})}{n!},

(65)

where
\mu^{\otimes n}\in(\mathbb{R}[L^{2}]^{*})^{\otimes n}\cong\mathbb{R}[L_{1}^{2},\ldots,L_{n}^{2}]^{*}
is defined by
\mu^{\otimes n}(f_{1}(L_{1})\dots f_{n}(L_{n}))=\mu(f_{1})\dots\mu(f_{n})
, extended for general even polynomials by linearity.
We often use the physicist notation

\displaystyle\mu^{\otimes n}(f)=\int f(\mathbf{L})\,\mathrm{d}{\mu(L_{1})}\dots\mathrm{d}{\mu(L_{n})},

(66)

which can be interpreted literally whenever
\mu
arises from a measure on
\mathbb{R}
with moments of arbitrary order.
In this notation we retrieve the formula (
9
) given in the introduction,

\displaystyle F_{g}[\mu]=\sum_{n\geq 3}\frac{1}{n!}\int V_{g,n}(\mathbf{L})\,\mathrm{d}{\mu(L_{1})}\dots\mathrm{d}{\mu(L_{n})}.

(67)

Note that
F_{g}[\mu]
only depends on the even moments
\int L^{2k}\mathrm{d}{\mu(L)}
, so we can formulate
F_{g}
as a function of the times
t_{k}[\mu]
defined as

\displaystyle t_{k}[\mu]=\int t_{k}(L)\mathrm{d}{\mu(L)}=\int\frac{2}{k!}\left(\frac{L}{2}\right)^{2k}\mathrm{d}{\mu(L)},

(68)

with
t_{k}(L)
as in Theorem
3
.
To avoid discussion about convergence, we interpret
F_{g}
as a multivariate formal power series, either in
7
7

7

In this interpretation, we will often drop the argument
[\mu]
in our notation.

(t_{0}[\mu],t_{1}[\mu],\ldots)
or in
\mu
.
The latter can be made more precise by fixing
\mu
and interpreting
F_{g}[x\mu]
as a univariate formal power series in
x
.

Denoting the delta measure at
L
by
\delta_{L}
, we can define the functional derivative
\frac{\delta}{\delta\mu(L)}
on such power series as

\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{\delta\mu(L)}P[\mu]=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial x}P[\mu+x\delta_{L}]\right|_{x=0},

(69)

which gives

\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{\delta\mu(L)}F_{g}[\mu]=\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{1}{n!}\int V_{g,1+n}(L,\mathbf{L})\,\mathrm{d}{\mu(L_{1})}\dots\mathrm{d}{\mu(L_{n})},

(70)

where we used the fact that
V_{g,n}
is symmetric.
We can relate this functional derivative to the formal power series in the times by

\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{\delta\mu(L)}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{2}{k!}\left(\frac{L}{2}\right)^{2k}\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{k}}.

(71)

Since we are dealing with genus
0
and require an origin cusp for the tree bijection, we will be mostly interested in

\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{\delta\mu(0)}F_{0}[\mu]=2\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{0}}F_{0}(t_{0},t_{1},\ldots)=\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{1}{n!}\int V_{0,n+1}(0,\mathbf{L})\,\mathrm{d}{\mu(L_{1})}\dots\mathrm{d}{\mu(L_{n})}.

(72)

Theorem
3
then implies

\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{\delta\mu(0)}F_{0}[\mu]=\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{T}}_{n}}\prod_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})}\frac{2^{\deg(\mathsf{v})-2}}{(\deg(\mathsf{v})-1)!}\,\gamma_{\deg(\mathsf{v})-1}\prod_{\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})}\frac{2^{\deg(\mathsf{b})-2}}{(\deg(\mathsf{b})-1)!}\,t_{\deg(\mathsf{b})-1}[\mu].

(73)

In order to exhibit an equation for the generating function, it is convenient to distinguish another cusp, besides the origin, that we may use to root the tree.
We therefore consider the generating function
R[\mu]
of the twice-punctured sphere, defined by

\displaystyle R[\mu]=\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta\mu(0)^{2}}F_{0}[\mu]=\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n!}\int V_{0,n+2}(0,0,\mathbf{L})\,\mathrm{d}{\mu(L_{1})}\dots\mathrm{d}{\mu(L_{n})}.

(74)

It follows from (
73
) that

\displaystyle R[\mu]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{T}}_{1+n}}\prod_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})}\frac{2^{\deg(\mathsf{v})-2}}{(\deg(\mathsf{v})-1)!}\,\gamma_{\deg(\mathsf{v})-1}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})\\
\mathsf{b}\neq\text{root}\end{subarray}}\frac{2^{\deg(\mathsf{b})-2}}{(\deg(\mathsf{b})-1)!}\,t_{\deg(\mathsf{b})-1}[\mu]

(75)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{T}}^{\text{unlab}}}\prod_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})}\frac{2^{\deg(\mathsf{v})-2}}{(\deg(\mathsf{v})-1)!}\,\gamma_{\deg(\mathsf{v})-1}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})\\
\mathsf{b}\neq\text{root}\end{subarray}}\frac{2^{\deg(\mathsf{b})-2}}{(\deg(\mathsf{b})-1)!}\,t_{\deg(\mathsf{b})-1}[\mu],

(76)

where
\widetilde{\mathfrak{T}}^{\text{unlab}}
is the set of bicolored plane trees with inner vertices of degree at least
3
and white vertices (unlabeled this time) of any degree and rooted on a white leaf.

Proof of Corollary
4
.

Since
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{T}}^{\text{unlab}}
admits a bijective decomposition at the vertex adjacent to the root, which may be an inner vertex of degree
d+1\geq 3
or a boundary vertex with degree
d+1\geq 1
, we immediately deduce the string equation

\displaystyle R[\mu]

\displaystyle=\sum_{d\geq 2}\frac{2^{d-1}}{d!}\gamma_{d}\,R[\mu]^{d}+\sum_{d\geq 0}\frac{2^{d-1}}{d!}t_{d}[\mu]R[\mu]^{d}.

(77)

To see that is solves
Z(R[\mu];\mu]=0
, it suffices to expand the Bessel functions,

\displaystyle Z(r;\mu]

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{r}}{\sqrt{2}\pi}J_{1}\left(2\pi\sqrt{2r}\right)-\int I_{0}\left(L\sqrt{2r}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{\mu(L)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{d\geq 1}\frac{2^{d-1}}{d!}(-1)^{d+1}\frac{\pi^{2d-2}}{(d-1)!}r^{d}-\sum_{d\geq 0}\frac{2^{d-1}}{d!}\int\frac{2}{d!}\left(\frac{L}{2}\right)^{2d}\mathrm{d}\mu(L)

\displaystyle=r-\sum_{d\geq 2}\frac{2^{d-1}}{d!}\gamma_{d}\,r^{d}-\sum_{d\geq 0}\frac{2^{d-1}}{d!}t_{d}[\mu]r^{d}.

∎

5
Geodesic distances in triply cusped spheres

So far we have used the tree bijection to compute the Weil–Petersson volume of
\mathcal{M}_{0,n+1}(0,\mathbf{L})
, which amounts to integrating the constant function against the measure
\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathrm{WP}}
.
More generally, one may try to integrate any statistic
f:\mathcal{M}_{0,n+1}(0,\mathbf{L})\to\mathbb{R}
against
\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathrm{WP}}
by considering the corresponding function

\displaystyle f\circ\mathsf{Glue}(\mathfrak{t},\cdot):\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})\to\mathbb{R}

(78)

on the polytope
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\mathbf{L})
for
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}(\mathbf{L})
.
Moreover, if
f\circ\mathsf{Glue}(\mathfrak{t},\cdot)
extends to an integrable function
f_{\mathfrak{t}}:\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t},\emptyset}^{\mathrm{anti}}(\mathbf{L})\to\mathbb{R}
on the larger polytope without Delaunay conditions on the angles, then the inclusion-exclusion principle of Section
4.1
shows that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{M}_{0,1+n}(0,\mathbf{L})}f(\mathsf{X})\mathrm{d}\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathrm{WP}}(\mathsf{X})=\sum_{\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{n}(\mathbf{L})}\sum_{\mathsf{A}\subset\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})}(-1)^{|\mathsf{A}|}\int_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t},\mathsf{A}}^{\mathrm{anti}}(\mathbf{L})}f_{\mathfrak{t}}(\lambda)\mathrm{d}\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\lambda),

(79)

where
\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{t}}
is the properly normalized Lebesgue measure of Lemma
19
.

In this section we focus on the distance difference
D:\mathcal{M}_{0,3+n}(0,0,0,\mathbf{L})\to\mathbb{R}
.
Recall from the introduction that
D(\mathsf{X})
is defined as
D(\mathsf{X})=d_{\mathsf{X}}(h_{0},h_{1})-d_{\mathsf{X}}(h_{0},h_{2})
in terms of the unit-length horocycles
h_{0},h_{1},h_{2}
around the first three cusps, where we have changed the indices to be compatible with the labeling of the tree
\mathfrak{t}
.
We will first make sure
D\circ\mathsf{Glue}(\mathfrak{t},\cdot)
can be reasonably expressed in terms of the decoration of the spine
\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{2+n}(0,0,\mathbf{L})
.
Note that in this case
h_{0}
is the horocycle around the origin and
h_{1},h_{2}
are horocycles associated to the boundary vertices with labels
1
and
2
of
\mathfrak{t}
.

5.1
Distances in terms of tree decoration

Given an edge
\mathsf{e}\in\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})
and a point
x\in\mathsf{X}
on this edge, we let
\alpha\in(0,\pi/2]
be the angle between
\mathsf{e}
and any of the two shortest geodesic from
x
to the origin.
Then we define the
edge distance
to be

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{e}}=d_{\mathsf{X}}(x,h_{0})+\log\sin\alpha.

(80)

It follows from hyperbolic trigonometry that this is independent of the choice of the point
x
, and that
d_{\mathsf{e}}
is half the length of the unique geodesic arc starting and ending at the origin dual to
\mathsf{e}
.
In case
\mathsf{e}
intersects this arc,
d_{\mathsf{e}}=d_{\mathsf{X}}(\mathsf{e},h_{0})
.

If
\mathsf{e}\in\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})
is incident to a boundary vertex corresponding to a cusp with unit-length horocycle
h_{i}
, then it is easily verified that its edge distance agrees with the distance from
h_{i}
to the origin,

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{e}}=d_{\mathsf{X}}(h_{i},h_{0}).

(81)

Therefore, if we let
\mathsf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{e}_{k}
for some
k\geq 1
be the unique sequence of edges connecting the first two boundary vertices in
\mathfrak{t}
, then

\displaystyle D(\mathsf{X})=d_{\mathsf{e}_{1}}-d_{\mathsf{e}_{k}}=\sum_{k=1}^{k-1}(d_{\mathsf{e}_{k}}-d_{\mathsf{e}_{k+1}}).

(82)

If
\mathsf{e}_{k}=\mathsf{e}_{\mathsf{v},1}
and
\mathsf{e}_{k+1}=\mathsf{e}_{\mathsf{v},2}
share an inner vertex
\mathsf{v}
, then the formula (
80
) with
x
at the position of
\mathsf{v}
implies that

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{e}_{k}}-d_{\mathsf{e}_{k+1}}=\log\sin\varphi(\mathsf{e}_{\mathsf{v},1})-\log\sin\varphi(\mathsf{e}_{\mathsf{v},2}).

(83)

Similarly, we will see that for two consecutive edges sharing a boundary vertex
\mathsf{b}
, the difference
d_{\mathsf{e}_{k}}-d_{\mathsf{e}_{k+1}}
can be expressed in terms of the boundary vertex decoration
(w_{\mathsf{b},j},v_{\mathsf{b},j})_{j=1}^{\deg(\mathsf{b})}
.

In the following we will concentrate on the function
f(\mathsf{X})=\exp(2uD(\mathsf{X}))
for fixed
u\in\mathbb{R}
of sufficiently small absolute value.
By (
83
), the formula extends to an integrable function
f_{\mathfrak{t}}:\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t},\emptyset}^{\mathrm{anti}}(0,0,\mathbf{L})\to\mathbb{R}
because the angles
\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}})
are restricted to
(0,\pi)
.
We may thus invoke the integration formula (
79
).

To evaluate the right-hand side we combine the factorization of
f_{\mathfrak{t}}
with that of the polytope
\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t},\mathsf{A}}^{\mathrm{anti}}(0,0,\mathbf{L})
from Lemma
21
.
Denoting by
\mathsf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{v}_{m}\in\mathsf{V}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})\cup\mathsf{B}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})
the vertices of the contracted tree
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}
encountered along the path between the first two boundary vertices, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}_{\mathfrak{t},\mathsf{A}}^{\mathrm{anti}}(\mathbf{L})}f_{\mathfrak{t}}(\lambda)\mathrm{d}\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{t}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})\\
\mathsf{v}\notin\{\mathsf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{v}_{m}\}\end{subarray}}\frac{2^{\deg{\mathsf{v}}-2}\pi^{2\deg(\mathsf{v})-4}}{(2\deg(\mathsf{v})-4)!}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})\\
\mathsf{b}\notin\{\mathsf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{v}_{m}\}\end{subarray}}\frac{2^{\deg{\mathsf{v}}-1}(L_{\mathsf{b}}/2)^{2\deg(\mathsf{b})-2}}{((\deg(\mathsf{b})-1)!)^{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}E_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}(u),

where
E_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}(u)
is the integral of
\exp(2u(d_{\mathsf{e}}-d_{\mathsf{e}^{\prime}}))
, involving the edge distances of the entrance edge
\mathsf{e}
and the exit edge
\mathsf{e}^{\prime}
, over the polytope
\mathcal{A}_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}
.

Figure 23:

Suppose
\mathsf{v}_{i}\in\mathsf{B}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})
is a boundary vertex of degree
\deg(\mathsf{v}_{i})=k\geq 2
.
Suppose the exit edge
\mathsf{e}^{\prime}
is the
\ell
th edge, for
2\leq\ell\leq k
, if we start counting from the entrance edge
\mathsf{e}
in clockwise order around
\mathsf{v}_{i}
.
Then
E_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}(u)
depends only on
k
,
\ell
and the associated boundary length
L_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}
,

\displaystyle E_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}(u)=E_{k,\ell}(L_{\mathsf{v}_{i}},u).

(84)

Suppose otherwise that
\mathsf{v}_{i}\in\mathsf{V}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})
is an anti-Delaunay vertex of degree
\deg(\mathsf{v}_{i})=k\geq 3
.
Recall from Section
4.1
that there is an associated subtree
\mathfrak{t}_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}
with
k
leafs, of which two are distinguished as the entrance and exit leaf.
Then
E_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}(u)
depends only on this subtree
\mathfrak{t}_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}
with distinguished leafs,

\displaystyle E_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}(u)=E_{\mathfrak{t}_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}}(u).

(85)

Recalling the definition of
\hat{X}_{n}(u;\mathbf{L})
from (
17
) in the introduction, the integration formula (
79
) gives

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{n}(u;\mathbf{L})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{T}_{2+n}(0,0,\mathbf{L})}\sum_{\mathsf{A}\subset\mathsf{E}(\mathfrak{t})}\Bigg[(-1)^{|\mathsf{A}|}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})\\
\mathsf{v}\notin\{\mathsf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{v}_{m}\}\end{subarray}}\frac{2^{\deg(\mathsf{v})-2}\pi^{2\deg(\mathsf{v})-4}}{(2\deg(\mathsf{v})-4)!}

\displaystyle\mkern 200.0mu\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})\\
\mathsf{b}\notin\{\mathsf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{v}_{m}\}\end{subarray}}\frac{2^{\deg(\mathsf{v})-1}(L_{\mathsf{b}}/2)^{2\deg(\mathsf{b})-2}}{((\deg(\mathsf{b})-1)!)^{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}E_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}(u)\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{T}}_{2+n}(0,0,\mathbf{L})}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{V}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})\\
\mathsf{v}\notin\{\mathsf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{v}_{m}\}\end{subarray}}\frac{2^{\deg(\mathsf{v})-2}\gamma_{\deg(\mathsf{v})-1}}{(\deg(\mathsf{v})-1)!}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{b}\in\mathsf{B}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})\\
\mathsf{b}\notin\{\mathsf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{v}_{m}\}\end{subarray}}\frac{2^{\deg(\mathsf{b})-2}t_{\deg(\mathsf{b})-1}(L_{\mathsf{b}})}{(\deg(\mathsf{b})-1)!}\,\prod_{i=1}^{m}\tilde{E}_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}(u),

(86)

with the new notation

\displaystyle\tilde{E}_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}(u)=\begin{cases}E_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}(u)&\mathsf{v}_{i}\in\mathsf{B}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})\\
(-1)^{\deg(\mathsf{v}_{i})-3}\sum_{\mathfrak{t}_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}}E_{\mathfrak{t}_{\mathsf{v}_{i}}}(u)&\mathsf{v}_{i}\in\mathsf{V}(\tilde{\mathfrak{t}})\end{cases},

(87)

where the sum is over all plane binary trees with a number of leafs equal to
\deg(\mathsf{v}_{i})
.

5.2
Distance-dependent three-point function

Following the procedure in Section
4.2
, the sum (
86
) simplifies drastically at the level of the generating functions, because the contribution at each of the
m
vertices along the path is independent of the others,

\displaystyle\hat{X}(u;\mu]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{1}{n!}\int\hat{X}_{n}(u;\mathbf{L})\mathrm{d}\mu(L_{1})\cdots\mathrm{d}\mu(L_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\left(\hat{X}_{\mathrm{A}}(u,R[\mu])+\int\mathrm{d}\mu(L)\hat{X}_{\mathrm{B}}(L,u,R[\mu])\right)^{m},

(88)

where we introduced the distance-dependent generating functions
\hat{X}_{\mathrm{A}}(u,r)
and
\hat{X}_{\mathrm{B}}(L,u,r)
around an anti-Delaunay vertex and boundary vertex respectively via

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{\mathrm{A}}(u,r)\coloneqq\sum_{\mathfrak{t}}(-1)^{|\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})|-1}r^{|\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})|}E_{\mathfrak{t}}(u),\qquad\hat{X}_{\mathrm{B}}(L,u,r)\coloneqq\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}r^{k-2}E_{k,\ell}(L,u).

(89)

Here the sum in
\hat{X}_{\mathrm{A}}(u,r)
is over all plane binary trees with a marked entrance and exit leaf, and
\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})
denotes the non-leaf vertices of
\mathfrak{t}
.

In the next two subsections we will establish the following explicit formulas for
\hat{X}_{\mathrm{A}}(u,r)
and
\hat{X}_{\mathrm{B}}(L,u,r)
.

Proposition 22
.

For an anti-Delaunay vertex we have the formal power series identity

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{\mathrm{A}}(u,r)=1-\frac{2\pi u}{\sin(2\pi u)}\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{u^{2p}}{(2p+1)!!}\frac{\partial^{p+1}}{\partial r^{p+1}}\frac{\sqrt{r}}{\sqrt{2}\pi}J_{1}(2\pi\sqrt{2r}).

Proposition 23
.

For a boundary vertex we have the formal power series identity

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{\mathrm{B}}(L,u,r)=\frac{2\pi u}{\sin(2\pi u)}\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{u^{2p}}{(2p+1)!!}\frac{\partial^{p+1}}{\partial r^{p+1}}I_{0}(L\sqrt{2r}).

Before turning to their proofs, we can conclude Theorem
5
.

Proof of Theorem
5
.

Comparing the formulas in Proposition
22
and Proposition
23
with the definition (
13
) of
\eta(u;\mu]
, we find that

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{\mathrm{A}}(u,r)+\int\mathrm{d}\mu(L)\hat{X}_{\mathrm{B}}(L,u,r)=1-\frac{2\pi u}{\sin(2\pi u)}\eta(u;\mu].

(90)

Hence the sum in (
88
) evaluates to

\displaystyle\hat{X}(u;\mu]=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\left(1-\frac{2\pi u}{\sin(2\pi u)}\eta(u;\mu]\right)^{m}=\frac{\sin(2\pi u)}{2\pi u\,\eta(u;\mu]}

as claimed.
∎

5.3
Around an inner vertex

This subsection is devoted to proving Proposition
22
.
We start by identifying an explicit integral expression for
\hat{X}_{\mathrm{A}}(u,r)
.

Lemma 24
.

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{\mathrm{A}}(u,r)=-\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}\int\mathrm{d}A\prod_{i=1}^{p}\left(-4F(r,\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})\left(\frac{\sin\alpha_{i}}{\sin\beta_{i}}\right)^{2u}\right),

(91)

where
\mathrm{d}A=\mathrm{d}\alpha_{1}\mathrm{d}\beta_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}\alpha_{p}\mathrm{d}\beta_{p}
on
\{0<\alpha_{1}<\beta_{1}<\cdots<\alpha_{p}<\beta_{p}<\pi\}
and

\displaystyle F(r,\theta)=\frac{\sqrt{r}}{\sqrt{2}\theta}J_{1}(2\theta\sqrt{2r}).

(92)

Proof.

Let us start by giving an interpretation to the series
F(r,\theta)
.
From the proof of Lemma
21
it follows that the polytope of an anti-Delaunay tree with
k
inner vertices and angle
\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{0})=\theta
on the root instead of
\pi
, is related to simplex
\Delta^{(\theta)}_{2k+1}
of size
\theta
.
Therefore its volume (including the power of
2
in the normalization) is
2^{k}\theta^{2k}/(2k)!
.
Hence, the generating function of such trees with weight
r
per non-root leaf and weight
-1
per inner vertex is

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}\frac{(2k)!}{k!(k+1)!}2^{k}\frac{\theta^{2k}}{(2k)!}r^{k+1}=\frac{\sqrt{r}}{\sqrt{2}\theta}J_{1}(2\theta\sqrt{2r})=F(r,\theta).

(93)

Figure 24:
An example of a plane binary tree
\mathfrak{t}
with a marked entrance and exit, in this case with
p=4
vertices on the path.

Recall that we should sum over all plane binary trees
\mathfrak{t}
with a marked entrance and exit leaf.
Let
p\geq 1
be the number of inner vertices of
\mathfrak{t}
along the path from entrance to exit and let
\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{1},\ldots,\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{p}
be the edges making up the path in reverse order, pointing towards the exit (see Figure
24
).
Let further
\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{i}^{\prime}
be the oriented edge pointing away from the path and sharing its starting point with
\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{i}
.
Then we write for
i=1,\ldots,p
,

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}=\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{i}),\quad\beta_{i}=\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{i})+\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{i}^{\prime}).

(94)

From our discussion in the proof of Lemma
21
it follows that

\displaystyle 0<\alpha_{1}<\beta_{1}<\cdots<\alpha_{p}<\beta_{p}<\pi.

(95)

Moreover

\displaystyle\exp(2u(d_{\mathsf{e}}-d_{\mathsf{e}^{\prime}}))=\prod_{i=1}^{p}\left(\frac{\sin(\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{i})+\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{i}^{\prime}))}{\sin\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{i})}\right)^{2u}=\prod_{i=1}^{p}\left(\frac{\sin\beta_{i}}{\sin\alpha_{i}}\right)^{2u}.

(96)

Since we can bijectively encode
\mathfrak{t}
by the
p
-tuple of subtrees whose roots are
\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,p
, and those subtrees have angle
\varphi(\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{i}^{\prime})=\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i}
on their root, we find

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{\mathrm{A}}(u,r)=-\sum_{\mathfrak{t}}(-1)^{|\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})|}r^{|\mathsf{V}(\mathfrak{t})|}E_{\mathfrak{t}}(u)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{p}2^{p}\int\mathrm{d}A\prod_{i=1}^{p}\left(2F(r,\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})\left(\frac{\sin\beta_{i}}{\sin\alpha_{i}}\right)^{2u}\right),

(97)

where the factor of
2^{p}
is due to the normalization of the volume measure
\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{t}}
compared to
\mathrm{d}A
and the factor of
2
in the product takes into account that the subtrees can be on the left or the right of the path.
∎

To evaluate the integral in the previous lemma, we need the following two somewhat curious integration identities.

Lemma 25
.

For any
0\leq\alpha_{1}\leq\beta_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\alpha_{p}\leq\beta_{p}\leq\pi
and
u\in[0,1/2)
we have the identity

\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{d}\gamma\prod_{i=1}^{p}\left(\frac{\sin(\alpha_{i}-\gamma)}{\sin(\beta_{i}-\gamma)}\right)^{2u}\mathbf{1}_{\{\gamma\notin[\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}]\}}=\frac{\sin\left(2u\left[\pi-\sum_{i=1}^{p}(\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})\right]\right)}{\sin(2\pi u)}.

(98)

Proof.

Let’s assume
u\in(0,1/2)
, the case
u=0
being obvious. The function

g(z)=\prod_{i=1}^{p}\left(\frac{\sin(\alpha_{i}-z)}{\sin(\beta_{i}-z)}\right)^{2u}=\exp\left(2u\sum_{i=1}^{p}\log\left(\frac{\sin(\alpha_{i}-z)}{\sin(\beta_{i}-z)}\right)\right)

is
\pi
-periodic and holomorphic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq p\\
n\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}[\alpha_{i}+n\pi,\beta_{i}+n\pi]
. Therefore, the integral

\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}g(\gamma+iy)\mathrm{d}\gamma,\qquad y\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}

depends only on the sign of
y
.
Hence

\lim_{y\nearrow\infty}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{d}\gamma\frac{e^{2\pi iu}g(\gamma+iy)-e^{-2\pi iu}g(\gamma-iy)}{2i}=\lim_{y\searrow 0}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{d}\gamma\frac{e^{2\pi iu}g(\gamma+iy)-e^{-2\pi iu}g(\gamma-iy)}{2i}.

Since
g(\gamma\pm iy)\to e^{\pm 2ui\sum_{i=1}^{p}(\alpha_{i}-\beta_{i})}
as
y\to\infty
, the left-hand side equals

\sin\left(2u\left[\pi-\sum_{i=1}^{p}(\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})\right]\right).

If
\gamma\in[\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}]
then
g(\gamma+i0)=e^{-4\pi iu}g(\gamma-i0)
, so the integrand on the right-hand side vanishes along the cuts.
The integral therefore evaluates to

\frac{\sin(2\pi u)}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{d}\gamma\prod_{i=1}^{p}\left(\frac{\sin(\alpha_{i}-\gamma)}{\sin(\beta_{i}-\gamma)}\right)^{2u}\mathbf{1}_{\{\gamma\notin[\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}]\}},

which proves the claimed expression.
∎

Lemma 26
.

For any analytic function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and
u\in(-1/2,1/2)
we have

\displaystyle Z_{p}[f]

\displaystyle:=\int\mathrm{d}A\prod_{i=1}^{p}\left(\frac{\sin\alpha_{i}}{\sin\beta_{i}}\right)^{2u}f(\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{d}\theta_{0}\frac{\operatorname{sinc}\left(2\theta_{0}u\right)}{\operatorname{sinc}(2\pi u)}\frac{\theta_{0}^{p}}{p!}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{p}}\mathrm{d}\theta_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}\theta_{p}\delta\left(\pi-\sum_{i=0}^{p}\theta_{i}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{p}f(\theta_{i}),

(99)

where
\operatorname{sinc}(x)=\sin(x)/x
and
\mathrm{d}A=\mathrm{d}\alpha_{1}\mathrm{d}\beta_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}\alpha_{p}\mathrm{d}\beta_{p}
on
\{0<\alpha_{1}<\beta_{1}<\cdots<\alpha_{p}<\beta_{p}<\pi\}
.

Furthermore, if
f
has Laplace transform

\hat{f}(y)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\theta e^{-y\theta}f(\theta),

(100)

the previous expression can be summarized as the formal power series identity

\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}Z_{p}[f]=\frac{\pi}{\sin(2\pi u)}\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\arctan\left(\frac{2u}{y-\hat{f}(y)}\right)\right\}(\pi),

(101)

where
\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\hat{f}(y)\right\}(\theta)=f(\theta)
is the corresponding inverse Laplace transform.

Proof.

Due to the shift symmetry in the Lebesgue measure and the
\pi
-periodicity of the integrand, we have

\displaystyle Z_{p}[f]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi-\sum_{i=1}^{p}(\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})}\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{d}\gamma\int\mathrm{d}A\prod_{i=1}^{p}\left(\frac{\sin(\alpha_{i}-\gamma)}{\sin(\beta_{i}-\gamma)}\right)^{2u}f(\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})\mathbf{1}_{\{\gamma\notin[\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}]\}}.

(102)

We can now use Lemma
25
to give us

\displaystyle Z_{p}[f]

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}A\frac{\operatorname{sinc}\left(2u\left[\pi-\sum_{i=1}^{p}(\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})\right]\right)}{\operatorname{sinc}(2\pi u)}\prod_{i=1}^{p}f(\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{d}\theta_{0}\frac{\operatorname{sinc}\left(2u\theta_{0}\right)}{\operatorname{sinc}(2\pi u)}\int\mathrm{d}A\,\delta\left(\pi-\theta_{0}-\sum_{i=0}^{p}(\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})\right)\prod_{i=1}^{p}f(\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{d}\theta_{0}\frac{\operatorname{sinc}\left(2u\theta_{0}\right)}{\operatorname{sinc}(2\pi u)}\frac{\theta_{0}^{p}}{p!}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{p}}\mathrm{d}\theta_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}\theta_{p}\delta\left(\pi-\theta_{0}-\sum_{i=0}^{p}\theta_{i}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{p}f(\theta_{i}),

which proves the first equation.

Using the Laplace transform, we get

\displaystyle Z_{p}[f]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{d}\theta_{0}\frac{\operatorname{sinc}(2u\theta_{0})}{\operatorname{sinc}(2\pi u)}\frac{\theta_{0}^{p}}{p!}\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\hat{f}(y)^{p}\right\}(\pi-\theta_{0})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\theta_{0}\frac{\operatorname{sinc}(2u\theta_{0})}{\operatorname{sinc}(2\pi u)}\frac{\theta_{0}^{p}}{p!}\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{e^{-\theta_{0}y}\hat{f}(y)^{p}\right\}(\pi).

(103)

In particular, summing over
p
,

\displaystyle\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}Z_{p}[f]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\theta_{0}\frac{\operatorname{sinc}(2u\theta_{0})}{\operatorname{sinc}(2\pi u)}\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{e^{-\theta_{0}(y-\hat{f}(y))}\right\}(\pi)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{\sin(2\pi u)}\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\arctan\left(\frac{2u}{y-\hat{f}(y)}\right)\right\}(\pi),

(104)

where we used
\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-ax}\sin(bx)\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{x}}{x}=\arctan(b/a)
.
∎

We now have all the ingredients to prove proposition
22
.

Proof of proposition
22
.

Let us apply Lemma
26
to

\displaystyle f(\theta)=-4F(r,\theta)=\frac{-2\sqrt{2r}}{\theta}J_{1}(2\theta\sqrt{2r}),

(105)

because then, using Lemma
24
,

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{A}(u,r)=-\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}Z_{p}[f]=1-\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}Z_{p}[f].

(106)

The Laplace transform of
f
is

\displaystyle\hat{f}(y)=\int\mathrm{d}\theta e^{-y\theta}f(\theta)=y-\sqrt{y^{2}+8r}.

Using the series expansion

\displaystyle\arctan\left(\frac{2u}{y-\hat{f}(y)}\right)

\displaystyle=\arctan\left(\frac{2u}{\sqrt{y^{2}+8r}}\right)=\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{p}}{2p+1}(2u)^{2p+1}(y^{2}+8r)^{-p-1/2},

and the inverse Laplace transform

\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{(y^{2}+8r)^{-p-1/2}\right\}(\pi)=\left(\frac{\pi}{2\sqrt{2r}}\right)^{p}\frac{1}{(2p-1)!!}J_{p}(2\pi\sqrt{2r}),

we conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}Z_{p}[f]

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{\sin(2\pi u)}\mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{\arctan\left(\frac{2u}{\sqrt{y^{2}+8r}}\right)\right\}(\pi)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi u}{\sin(2\pi u)}\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{u^{2p}}{(2p+1)!!}\left(-\frac{\sqrt{2}\pi}{\sqrt{r}}\right)^{p}J_{p}(2\pi\sqrt{2r})

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi u}{\sin(2\pi u)}\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{u^{2p}}{(2p+1)!!}\frac{\partial^{p+1}}{\partial r^{p+1}}\frac{\sqrt{r}}{\sqrt{2}\pi}J_{1}(2\pi\sqrt{2r}).

(107)

Together with (
106
) this gives the claimed formula.
∎

5.4
Around a boundary vertex

We now analyse the distance differences when passing through a boundary vertex
\mathsf{b}
of degree
k\geq 2
.
To lighten notation in this section, we write
w_{j}=w_{\mathsf{b},j}
,
v_{j}=v_{\mathsf{b},j}
and
d_{j}=d_{\vec{e}_{\mathsf{b},j}}
for
j=1,\ldots,k
.
Moreover, we assume the ordering of edges around
\mathsf{b}
is chosen such that
\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{b},1}
is the entrance edge, connecting to boundary vertex
1
, and
\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{b},\ell}
is the exit edge, connecting to the boundary vertex with label
2
.
Recall that Proposition
23
involves the function
E_{k,\ell}(L,u)
given by the integral of
e^{2u(d_{1}-d_{\ell})}
over the polytope
(\Delta_{k}^{(L/2)})^{2}
.

Figure 25:
Left: illustration of the two ideal triangles spiralling into a boundary associated to
\mathsf{b}
of degree
\deg(\mathsf{b})=2
. Right: the relation between distances to the origin and the shears and half-shears. Note that
z_{\mathsf{c}}
is negative here, while
z_{\vec{\mathsf{e}}},z_{\vec{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}}
are positive.

Recall from Section
3.2
the construction of the half-shear
z_{\vec{\mathsf{e}}}
associated to an oriented edge
\vec{\mathsf{e}}
starting at a boundary vertex (Figure
17
), and the shear
z_{\mathsf{c}}
associated to a corner
\mathsf{c}
(Figure
19
).
If
\vec{\mathsf{e}}=\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{b},j}
and
\vec{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}=\vec{\mathsf{e}}_{\mathsf{b},j+1}
share a corner
\mathsf{c}=\mathsf{c}_{\mathsf{b},j}
the picture looks like that of Figure
25
.
Comparing to the indicated distances
d_{\mathsf{e}}=d_{j}
and
d_{\mathsf{e}^{\prime}}=d_{j+1}
to the origin horocycle, it should be clear that we have the relation

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{e}^{\prime}}+z_{\vec{\mathsf{e}}^{\prime}}=d_{\mathsf{e}}-z_{\vec{\mathsf{e}}}-z_{\mathsf{c}}.

(108)

Together with (
48
) and (
51
), this gives

e^{d_{j+1}-d_{j}}=e^{-w_{j+1}-w_{j}}e^{-z_{\mathsf{c}}}=e^{v_{j}}\frac{1-e^{-w_{j+1}}}{e^{w_{j}}-1}.

(109)

Taking products on both sides, we can compare
d_{\ell}
to
d_{1}
for
\ell=1,\ldots,k
,

e^{2u(d_{\ell}-d_{1})}=e^{2u\sum_{j=1}^{\ell-1}v_{j}}e^{-2u\sum_{j=2}^{\ell}w_{j}}\left(1-e^{-w_{\ell}}\right)^{2u}\left(1-e^{-w_{1}}\right)^{-2u}.

(110)

Thanks to the separation of variables we can express
E_{k,\ell}(L,u)
as

\displaystyle E_{k,\ell}(L,u)=2^{k-1}I_{k,\ell}(u,L)\tilde{I}_{k,\ell}(u,L),

(111)

where the factor
2^{k-1}
comes from the normalization of the measure
\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{t}}
and we have introduced

\displaystyle I_{k,\ell}(u,L)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Delta_{k}^{(L/2)}}\mathrm{d}v_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}v_{k-1}e^{2u\sum_{i=1}^{\ell-1}v_{i}}.

(112)

\displaystyle\tilde{I}_{k,\ell}(u,L)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Delta_{k}^{(L/2)}}\mathrm{d}w_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}w_{k-1}e^{-2u\sum_{i=2}^{\ell}w_{i}}\left(1-e^{-w_{\ell}}\right)^{-2u}\left(1-e^{-w_{1}}\right)^{2u}.

(113)

Proof of Proposition
23
.

Recall that we should compute

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{\mathrm{B}}(L,u,r)=\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}r^{k-2}E_{k,\ell}(L,u)=\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}r^{k-2}2^{k-1}I_{k,\ell}(u,L)\tilde{I}_{k,\ell}(u,L)

We can switch from integrating over the simplex to the full
\mathbb{R}^{k}_{>0}
by Laplace transforming,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}Le^{-Lz}I_{k,\ell}(u,L)

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}v_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}v_{k}e^{-2z\sum_{i=1}^{k}v_{i}+2u\sum_{i=1}^{\ell-1}v_{i}}

\displaystyle=2^{1-k}(z-u)^{1-\ell}z^{\ell-k-1}

(114)

\displaystyle=2^{1-k}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\binom{\ell+m-2}{m}u^{m}z^{-k-m}.

Taking the inverse Laplace tranform yields

\displaystyle I_{k,\ell}(u,L)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}u^{m}L^{k+m-1}\frac{2^{1-k}}{(k+m-1)!}\binom{\ell+m-2}{m}.

(115)

Similarly for
\tilde{I}_{k,\ell}(u,L)
the Laplace transform factorizes,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}Le^{-Lz}\tilde{I}_{k,\ell}(u,L)

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}w_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}w_{k}e^{-2z\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}-2u\sum_{i=2}^{\ell}w_{i}}\left(1-e^{-w_{\ell}}\right)^{-2u}\left(1-e^{-w_{1}}\right)^{2u}

\displaystyle=2^{3-k}(z+u)^{2-\ell}z^{\ell-k}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}w_{1}e^{-2zw_{1}}(1-e^{-w_{1}})^{2u}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}w_{\ell}e^{-2(z+u)w_{\ell}}(1-e^{-w_{\ell}})^{-2u}

\displaystyle=2^{3-k}(z+u)^{2-\ell}z^{\ell-k}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}x\,x^{2z-1}(1-x)^{2u}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}y\,y^{2(z+u)-1}(1-y)^{-2u}

\displaystyle=2^{3-k}(z+u)^{2-\ell}z^{\ell-k}\frac{\Gamma(2z)\Gamma(1+2u)}{\Gamma(2z+2u+1)}\,\frac{\Gamma(2z+2u)\Gamma(1-2u)}{\Gamma(2z+1)}

\displaystyle=2^{1-k}(z+u)^{1-\ell}z^{\ell-k-1}\Gamma(1+2u)\Gamma(1-2u),

(116)

where in the second-to-last equality we recognized Beta integrals.
Using the reflection formula for the Gamma function

\Gamma(1-2u)\Gamma(1+2u)=\frac{2\pi u}{\sin 2\pi u},

(117)

and comparing to (
114
), it follows immediately that

\tilde{I}_{k,\ell}(u,L)=\frac{2\pi u}{\sin 2\pi u}I_{k,\ell}(-u,L).

(118)

Summing over
\ell
, we find

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}I_{k,\ell}(u,L)\tilde{I}_{k,\ell}(u,L)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi}{u\sin 2\pi u}\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}u^{2}I_{k,\ell}(-u,L)I_{k,\ell}(u,L)

\displaystyle\!\!\mathrel{{\mathop{=}\limits}}\frac{2\pi 4^{1-k}L^{2k-4}}{u\sin 2\pi u}\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}u^{2p}L^{2p}\sum_{m=0}^{2p-2}\frac{(-1)^{m}}{(k+m-1)!}\binom{\ell+m-2}{m}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\frac{1}{(k+2p-m-3)!}\binom{\ell+2p-m-4}{2p-m-2}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi 4^{1-k}L^{2k-4}}{u\sin 2\pi u}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}u^{2p}L^{2p}2\frac{p!}{(2p)!(k-2)!(k+p-2)!},

(119)

where in the last equality we use the summation identity of Lemma
27
below.

Putting everything together we obtain

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{\mathrm{B}}(L,u,r)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}r^{k-2}2^{k-1}\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}I_{k,\ell}(u,L)\tilde{I}_{k,\ell}(u,L)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi u}{\sin(2\pi u)}\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}u^{2p-2}\frac{2^{p}p!}{(2p)!}\frac{r^{k-2}2^{2-k-p}L^{2(k+p-2)}}{(k-2)!(k+p-2)!}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi u}{\sin(2\pi u)}\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{u^{2p}}{(2p+1)!!}\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\frac{r^{k-2}2^{1-k-p}L^{2(k+p-1)}}{(k-2)!(k+p-1)!}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi u}{\sin(2\pi u)}\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{u^{2p}}{(2p+1)!!}\frac{\partial^{p+1}}{\partial r^{p+1}}\sum_{k=1-p}^{\infty}\frac{2^{1-k-p}L^{2(k+p-1)}r^{k+p-1}}{(k+p-1)!^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi u}{\sin(2\pi u)}\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\frac{u^{2p}}{(2p+1)!!}\frac{\partial^{p+1}}{\partial r^{p+1}}I_{0}(L\sqrt{2r}),

(120)

as claimed.
∎

Lemma 27
.

The following summation identity holds for every
p\geq 1
and
k\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\sum_{m=0}^{2p-2}(-1)^{m}\frac{\binom{\ell+m-2}{m}}{(k+m-1)!}\frac{\binom{\ell+2p-m-4}{2p-m-2}}{(k+2p-m-3)!}

\displaystyle=2\frac{p!}{(2p)!(k-2)!(p+k-2)!}.

Proof.

We give a proof based on known hypergeometric identities.
Performing the sum over
m
on the left-hand side we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\frac{(\ell+2p-4)!}{(\ell-2)!(2p-2)!(k+2p-2)!(k-1)!}{{}_{3}F_{2}}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}2p-2&3-k-2p&\ell-1\\
k&4-2p-\ell\end{smallmatrix};1\right],

where
{{}_{p}F_{q}}
is a generalized hypergeometric function.
According to
[
54
, equations (2.3.3.2) and (1.1.5)]
this hypergeometric function evaluates to

\displaystyle{{}_{3}F_{2}}

\displaystyle\left[\begin{smallmatrix}2p-2&3-k-2p&\ell-1\\
k&4-2p-\ell\end{smallmatrix};1\right]=\frac{(3-p-\ell)^{(\ell-2)}(1+k-\ell)^{(\ell-2)}}{(4-2p-\ell)^{(\ell-2)}(p+k-\ell)^{(\ell-2)}}{{}_{3}F_{2}}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}2p-2&3-k-2p&1\\
k&2-2p\end{smallmatrix};1\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{(3-p-\ell)^{(\ell-2)}(1+k-\ell)^{(\ell-2)}}{(4-2p-\ell)^{(\ell-2)}(p+k-\ell)^{(\ell-2)}}\left({{}_{2}F_{1}}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}3-k-2p&1\\
k\end{smallmatrix};1\right]-\frac{(3-k-2p)^{(2p-1)}}{k^{(2p-1)}}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}2-k&1\\
k+2p-1\end{smallmatrix};1\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{(3-p-\ell)^{(\ell-2)}(1+k-\ell)^{(\ell-2)}}{(4-2p-\ell)^{(\ell-2)}(p+k-\ell)^{(\ell-2)}}\frac{2(k-1)}{2p+2k-4},

(121)

where
a^{(n)}=a(a+1)(a+2)\dots(a+n-1)
is the rising factorial.
With this the desired sum becomes

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\frac{(\ell+2p-4)!}{(\ell-2)!(2p-2)!(k+2p-2)!(k-1)!}\frac{(3-p-\ell)^{(\ell-2)}(1+k-\ell)^{(\ell-2)}}{(4-2p-\ell)^{(\ell-2)}(p+k-\ell)^{(\ell-2)}}\frac{2(k-1)}{2p+2k-4}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}\frac{(k+p-\ell-1)!(p+\ell-3)!}{(k-\ell)!(\ell-2)!(p-1)!(p+k-2)!(2p+k-3)!}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(p+k-2)!(2p+k-3)!(k-2)!}{{}_{2}F_{1}}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}p+1&-k\\
-k+p\end{smallmatrix};1\right]=2\frac{p!}{(p+k-2)!(2p)!(k-2)!},

(122)

as claimed.
∎
